PRACTICAL POULTRY

FARMING.

[BY REDCAP.]

FOREWORD.

Seven months have elapsed since we talked poultry matters. And in the poultry world much of special interest has happened. Counting from July last the gteat events of the season have come and gone. Altrincham, Hayward's Heath, the London Dairy, Crystal Palace and Birmingham Shows have all been held. These great exhibitions are always of special interest to lovers of poultry. For it is in such places; that we become acquainted with all that is new and up-to-date in poultry matters. It is here also that the product of the fancier's ingenuity and breeding skill is tested, the value of the many and different varieties of poultry is ascertained, for according to a breed's popularity in the show pen, so also is its popularity determined in the general field of the poultry industry. Therefore, inasmuch as it is always wise to take up popular breeds, the time will not Le lost if we

===== 0002601_19130208_art0005.txt | included=true | included =====
REVIEW THE OPERATIONS OF 1012.

Let us see how the different breeds and varieties have fared during the past year. This will perhaps guide those about to commence in the industry of poultry farming. A very pleasing feature and one that as often been advocated by the writer, is the revival of some of the older breeds cf poultry. The old English Dorking has been very much to the front of late, Andalusians are now able to 'crow' a little louder, Brahmas and Cochins have been in extra demand; Scotch Greys and Scotch Dumpies have revived, Redcaps have made god pi ices, and last but not least is the overseas demand for specimens of the good old Spanish. These are all healthy signs. Foreign demand is a sure sign in the revival of any breed. When one hears of inquiries from abroad, it usually follows that a greater interest in home production is enlivened.

===== 0002601_19130208_art0006.txt | included=true | included =====
NEWER VARIETIES.

And what of the newer larieties Well! These also have more than held their own. Orpingtons are as popular as ever: perhaps the balance of popularity has been on the side of Whites. Wyandottes as a breed, including all varieties, are, I think, bred in larger numbers than most other breeds of poultry. I doubt if the Plymouth Rock will ever Ns aue, they have been in the forefront now for very many years and the past season has been no expection, inasmuch as the highest trophy at the Crystal Palace was awarded to a Barred Rock hen. Buff Rocks are also on the increase, have never had a better year; and j.alous of their, ' brethren ' the Blues are making a bid for popularity. Houdhans, Langshans, Leghorns,Faverolles, Minorca, Campines, Rhode Island Reds, and old English game have all experienced a fair share of the success of the past season. To those who are about to make a start I may say that, they cannot get far wrong in selecting any or all of the breeds aforementioned. For well bred stock there is a ready demand both at home and abroad.

===== 0002601_19130208_art0007.txt | included=true | included =====
TEE COMING SEASON.

And what of the future? The success of my readers and the success of our poultry column, depends absolutely on what use we make of each other. Readers seem to forget that this column is absolutely and specially set aside for those interested in poultry. Therefore, why not make more of the advantage offered you? Penny stamps ale still the same price, and this, after all, is the total cost to you. Send along your queries; send along your suggestions for all will be freely and fairly considered. Last year, you will remember, I gave an illustration of a different breed each week accompanied by a description if each illustration. I do not propose a renewal of this idea unless otherwise desired by my readers. However, if any of you think that it would be helpful I will certainly accede to your request. I should be glad to have your views in this matter as early in the season as possible. PRACTICAL ADVICE GRATIS.

As far as lies in my power, and when convenient, I will render to you personal practical advice. Already one of my readers has asked me to pay him a vist with a view to the laying out of a small poultry farm. I have willingly consented to do so and when in posses- sion of my observatioas from the visit I will give you the benefit thereof. Thus I may he able to "kill two birds with one stone" In this manner we shall undoubtedly prove our mutual interests. Now please do make some use of the poultry column. Send your queries written in ink and use only one side of the paper and address them to "Redcap," c/o "The Warrington Ex aminer." Let all correspondence reach me not later than Tuesday morning of each week.

Eggs, Poultry, etc.

===== 0002601_19130208_art0008.txt | included=true | included =====
FEBRUARY.

Keen froFts are still probable, cold winds continue to blow, the ground is very fat with moisture, and to say the least it is not much like chicken-rearing weather. Nevertheless, it is high time to make a start. Granted that you are about to put down a few eggs, three weeks must elapse before the chicks arrive and during that period the weather might be much more favourable. Those who want a winter egg- producing stock must not lose much time in putting down a few eggs from the heavy breeds. If you have not already mated up the pens I would advise you to do so without delay. MATING THE PENS.

My remarks on this subject at this season of the year must apply only to the heavy breeds. You will be quite early enough with the lighter breeds three weeks hence. In mating up then, I would not advise the use of more than three or four females to one male per pen. Eggs are not so easily fertilized early in the season. It is therefore useless to pen together in larger numbers. The selection of the male bird is very important. He must be vigorous and fully developed. Never use a male bird less than 12 months old, and if mated to pullets of last year a second year cock is an essential to various progeny. FEEDING THE BREEDING STOCK.

Special care should be used in feeding the breeding stock. In your anxiety for a large egg supply avoid forcing condiments such as pepper, cayenne and all

WORKINGMEN AND THE INFIRMARY.

INSURANCE ACT AND WEEKLY CONTRIBUTIONB.

A meeting of the employes of Messrs W D Houghton and Sons, Sankey Bridges, was held at noon on Thurs- day, to consider the question of a systematic contribir• tion to the Infirmary from each employe of the firm. Addresses were delivered on the work of the Institution t,y, Mr Roger C Parr (hon treasurer), Alderman Burton (chairman of the Workingmen's Infirmary Committee), and Mr .1 H 3 Hampson (the secretary). Alderman Burton said they were at the present time passing through a very trying period financially. He was pleased to say, however, that the good work wee still being continued and was on the increase, but he regretted the subscriptions were declining. That was a serious state of affairs and if they were to regal d it from a business point of view, they would see it could only end in liquidation or the Bankruptcy Court. That, however, could not be so with the Infirmary as they had their endowments but it would lead to an unfortunate state of things as the Board of Management would have to narrow down its usefulness in order to meet the reducea income. He was sure they would all be sorry to see such a thing occur and the Board of Management would like to see the employees of the various works in the town adopt a system of weekly subscriptions, no matter how small the amonnt each workman paid. At Monks Hall and Co., they discussed the matter with the men who one and all agreed to a small Bum being deducted from their wages each week. That was kept on at the present day and a good amount was handed to the Infirmary. Some months ago they heard the police force was contributing scarcely any- thing to the Infirmary. The Chief Constable called the men together and they all agreed to pay a weekly sum to the Institution. They would never miss a small sum weekly and they would be helping to benefit a noble institution.

Mr Hampson said the meeting had been called at the request of one of their representatives on the Working- men's Committee. The Infirmary was managed on business lines by the Board of Management which con- sisted of business men, representatives of the Working- men's Committee and ministers of all denominations who worked together for the benefit of the sick and suffering, it was non-political and undenominational. , In 1911 they treated 938 in-patients who were nursed, fed and had the attendance of the, medical staff. They also treated 1,163 home patients 4,371 out patients and 5,507 accidents, and 544 opera- tions were performed. The expenditure was £4,916 and the income about £1,418. Although they were about £560 short, not a single patient was stinted in anything. Insured persons would want attendance for accidents and operations as before, and their wives and children would also need treatment. Only a small pro- portion of patients treated last year would be insured persons. Ile hoped they would consider the remarks of Alderman Burton who had worked for the Infirmary for thirty years. and decide to pay a small sum each week. All over the town employers had decided to go on as before, and in some cases had increased their subscrip- tions, but in no single instance had they decided to dis- continue owing to the Insurance Act, Two splendid meetings had been held at the works of Messrs Richmond, at which Mr Ransome had given very material help to the Infirmary by his support. Mr Roger C Parr said he should like to thank Mr Houghton for allowing them to address them. At the present day one had to subscribe to a good many things, but he thought there was none which deserved it more than the Infirmary. Only one-third of the persons treated at the institution were insured persons, and the other two-thirds were not touched by the Act. That showed how important it was that they should continue to help the Infirmary to help to carry on its good work, and he hoped they would fall in with tete suggestion. (Applause). A vote of thanks was accorded to Mr john Houghton for his kindndess in allowing the meeting to be held, on the proposition of Alderman Burton, seconded by Mr Roger C Parr. A further meeting of the men will be held to consider the best method for carrying out the suggestion.

LIFE'S STRUGGLE.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 8, 1913.

===== 0002601_19130208_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
PROFESSOR HOBHOUSE ON THE CO-OPERATIVE TENDENCY.

Politics and "The Liberty of the Subject."

The relation between the science of life and the art of the statesman formed the subject of a brilliant address by Professor L. T. Hobhouse, of London University, in the Co-operative Hall on Monday evening, The Professor is famed the world over as an expoeent of sooial ehilaeophy. and thanks are due to the Literary and Philosephical Society for giving a Warrington audience the oppertunity of hearing him. It was encouraging to see a large attendance, aud still more notable was the thoughtful iaterest with which the discourse was f ,flowed. Mr. Oswald Loft- house presided, and rightly forecasted that the Professor would not deal with politics in the nari ow party meaning which now clung to the world, hut in the finer, nobler and broader sense which wae current in earlier times. As Professor Hobhouse put it, his subject "Biology and Politics" presented nothing very exciting, tbrili- ing or romantic, bat he succeeded in making it interesting even to those who can lay claim to no special knowledge of scientific methods es applied to politics. He pessesses a remarkable flow of language and, without the aid of a note, spoke with consistent rapidity for fully an hour. The 'Professor discussed the doctrine of unmitigated competition and the survival of the Ilttest,as contrasted with the ethical and moral ideal of co-operatien and human eqiielit v. His views may be tersely summed up in his concluding passages. What, ho asked, was to he our attitude as to the relation between the biological and the sociological conceptions of society ? What was to be our notion of progress ? Was it to be a notion of advance through a process of canflict weed- ing out those who were fit to survive and throwieg the rest inlo the dustbin? Or WhEi it to be through the development of the ethical religious mind, the wiriening and deepening of the more careful, subtle and close application of those notions of hemen brotherhood, freedom and tcpiality, loading us in the direction of a free co-operative common- wealth, extending uliimately in its ramifications over the whole of mankind? Ho believed it was in the latter direci ion that our efforts were to be turned. It was the ideal towards whice thorough-going, philoso- phical analysis of ethics pointed as being the true good of man and, on the whole, the history of evolution supported it. It was true that our progress, lamentably small as it, had been, presented the world to us still as the theatre of much conflict and sometimes of cruel internecine strife. But still it did show us. growing up within this world which was se eevoid of peace and harmony, centres of c ,eiperetion, solidarity, mutual aid and friendliness, which extended as we advanced through successive stages of the organic world to man and from barbaric man to the Lighest form of civiliza- tion. It shoeed us that the principle of harmony, in opposition to the priticiple of conflict, had been justified by the ultimate arbiter to which tne theory of conflict appealed—the arbiter of success. Earlier in his lecture, the professor stated that the impulse of society, as expressed in the Poor Law, was gradually becoming more eurnane and resulted in greater care fer the less fortunate in the community. But a doubt was arising as to the propriety of this, and the doubt derived a certain amount of support from the biological teaching that progress in life was achieved by the success of the fit and the elimination of the unfit. The thinkers of this school asked, `• If we arrest this beneficent process are we not laving up for the race a heritage of evil ? " The line of argument was undoubtedly one of the intellectual ,orces that was most relied upon by those who were oppos- ing measures for the extinction of poverty and destitution by collective action. They were not hard- hearted, and they no doubt believed that they were taking a long view and acting for the good or the race. The Mental Deficiency Bill was an example of the way in which these ideas as to the necessity of dealing with the unfit by extinguishing them as painlessly as possible had had a direct effect qp intended legislation. He had much sympathy with the basal idea or the bill, which was to provide institutions where the unfit among the poor could be taken from their miserable life outside and treated humanely, as the cases demanded. The professor turned special attention, however, to those parts of the Bill which were desisned to prevent the feeble-minded from bringing children into the world. He pointed out that the definition of. feeble-minded persons was those who.„were " incapable of competing on equal terms with their normal fellows or of managine, themselves and their affairs with ordinary prudence." He thought it was possible that there were some men,quite normally and healthily con- stituted, who had occasionally been attracted by a pretty young woman who could not altogether satise factorily pass a test of the above stringent character. (Laualiter.) He was not picking out young women as being feeble-minded in comparison with young men,' but he was e,iving them credit for rather more sense in their selections. (Laughter ) A woman was not likely to marry a young man who was unable to compete with his fellows, unless his circumstances were such that he had no need to compete. (Laughter.) It therefore became an enormous advantage to secure as partner in the race of life a young man who had great passessions. Tt must, boivever, he remembered that anyone entering into matriineny with a person who could not be certified as capable of earning his or her own living on equal terms, was committing a misdemeanour and was liable to imprisonment with hard labour for not more than two years. (Laughter.) Anybody who aided and abetted in such a marriage was liable to a similar penalty. rhe PitoFessou said he had a perfectly open mind about the Eugenist argument that there were certain elements of unfitness in .he stock which were heredi- tary, and which could only be ended by the prohibition of parentage, but civilised methods must be used We could not resort to methods of barbarism. If the student of biology could define those instances more, and show more clearly whet taints were hereditable, and in what stocks they existed, he would undoubtedly be supplying important data, but when they came to com- pulsion in these matters they had to be exceedingly certain that the at ick they were seeking to eliminate was in the main bad. An interesting discussion followed the lecture. Mr A .1 JOLLEY, who moved a vote of thanks to Pro- fessor Hobhouse, said he seemed to detect in the professor's address something of the old cry " You must not interfere with the liberty of the subject." Thank heaven, ye had got a good deal past that now. Much of the real progress [lade in the last forty years had been due to definite and well-considered restrictions and restraints upon individuals. In every organized progressive community there must be the tendency to further restrictions, espeoially with regatd to the control of the unfortunate class of persons with whom it was proposed to deal in the Mental Deficiency Bill. If they carried out to its logical consequences the plea for unrestricted freedom, it would mean that they must liberate all the persons at present in lunatic asylums and allow them to inter- marry and to. reproduce objectionable types. As a matter of fact, they had already restrained certain types, and the only point in practical politics to-day was : What is to be the next step in the restriction of the reproduction of certain pronounced and recognized unsuitable types—types unfit to be at large, or at least to increase in number,in P. civilised community ? It was quite true that in recent years legislation had tended to prevent that internecine competition in industrial matters which used to obtain and which was the ideal of the orthodox Manchester school. Again, thank heaven we had got past that long since. That was another direc- tion in which legislative restraint Lad been usefully imposed to ensure real progress towards a higher and better civilisation, but at the same time they could not have progress without some competition. Mr C S BUIZGEsS seconded the vote of thanks.

Mr C DuxEs said the Drofessor had not made stifticiently clear what he meant by "the unfit." His (the speaker's) view was that we wore already eliminating not only the unfit, viewed from an ethical standpoint, but many desirable persons. Industrial competition resulted in pressing down people who had greater co-operative or altruistic instincts than most of those who based their conception of progress on the ability to crush down the individual. What was to be the standard of efficiency ? The tendency of the modern workshop was to nervous breakdown and insanity and the great question was how to remove the economic barriers to the development of philosophical views ? Dr MANSON said that in his criticism of the cruder aspects of biological reasoning as applied to society, Professor Hobhouse represented the old antagonism which existed between the medical and the legal pro- fessions. In the study of CT iwinolopyy, they found nftt, the doctor was nearly always on the side of the criminal. HD regarded the criminal as a patient to be treated, while the lawyer said he was a felon to be punished. Biology could be reconciled to the highest ethical standsrds,and the better they knew biology the more they would incline to that view. The proceedings in refer- ence to the Mental Defic,en.. 7 Bill had shown that the medical profeesion was going to take an important part in pelitics. Hitherto the physician had not adjusted himself to his proper place in ilia political sphere, but a change was coming. If the biological view, when properly understood,mede for human solidarity it made also for the peace of the world. They were discover- ing that there were common enemies of mankind which must be fought. Professor HbMIOUSE,in reply, said he was no extreme partisan of the voluntary principle but he was in favour of trying it before resorting to compulsion. He would like to see what could be clone by providing suitable institutions in sufficient numbers for the mentally deficient and applying compulsion in cases wbere it was manifestly called for. If they could show him a way of preserving full scope for personal liberty and initiative and at the same time getting rid of the com- petitive element, he would say they had pointed the

===== 0002601_19130208_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
ECONOMICS IN POLITICS.

LECTURE TO JUNIOR LIBERALS.

"Economics in Politics " was the subject of an interesting lecture given under the auspices of the Junior Lioeral Association, in the Horsernarket-street Chambers on Monday evening, by Mr W Abbot, M.A., of Altrinchiata. There was a fair attendance of mem- bers, Mr 0 Toulmin presiding. Mr Abbot treated his sebject very skilfully and provided the audieace with much good food for thougut. In his opening renmearks the CHAIRMAN' said the subject upon which Mr Abbot bad come to speak was a very oiflicult one for those who hid a rather elementary knowledge of politics and political econ imy. In the first place he believed the word "economy " meant the management of the concerns of a household or in a broader eerie " the judicious and frugal management of public affairs." It was as well to judge our political faith by its economic value, and incidentally to put the faith of opponents to the same test. The Free Trade ' crusade which was started by Cobden and Bright gave a very striking illustration of the economic truths that were behind that cause. Before the repeal of tee Cora Laws, the people of this country were suffering from chronic poverty and thousands were on the verge of starvation for want of loud. That sort of thing was brought about by the Government of the day refusing to recognise the economic fact that a tariff imposed on foreign food did not make Us richer, because it limited the supply of our food and at the same time increased the price. A tariff that increased the price of an article, instead of increasing our wealth, impoverished us, because the exchange value of the articles that ire manufactured became less, for the reason that as a result of the tariff we would have to pay more for everything we bought. If we limited the areas of our trade by building what Tariff Reformers called a tariff wall round the Empire, we were impoverishing ourselves still further by discouraging the development of the natural resources or the useful articles of countries outside the Empire which provided raw material for British industries. On the question of tariffs the Tory Party failed to think economically. Ile (the speaker), had never yet seen a leaflet or book with the title -The economics of Tariff Reform." He would be very much surprised if such a document existed because the whole thing was economically ulisound. (Hear, hear.) In conclusi n the Chairmen said the land question gave another striking example of the advieamiity of juagiue a subject on its economic value. They were told by John Stuart Mill " that every article which was produced came from the land." d hat being a fact which could not be contradicted, was it not economically true to say that a land system which did not allow the land to be put to the best use, was unsound ? (Hear, hear.) In the course i an interesting and educational lecture, Mr AIIBOT said there was a large amount of prejudice against economics. Way was the science of economics such a diilicutt one ? There were various reasons, but he thought ihey could trace them.till to the same thing—thet it dealt with everynay occur- rences and it uad to use everyday language and terms, such as wages, rent, interest, Nee If they , examined any one of those terms, they found many different meanings were attached to them. If they asked the man in tire street what rent WPS, he said it was what he paid for the privilege of liyiug in a house. Economic rent was, however, the payment a person made for any advantages which he secured over and above the advantages he got for not paying. With their definitions in regard to economics they bad to be very careful, and if they used a term in a sense which might have two or three different meanings they had to define which one they mourn. There was another diffi- culty, and that was because economics dealt with such common, every-day things that everyone, whether trained or or not, thought himself capable, after a minute's thought, of giving a decision on even the most intricate point in economics because the subjects were so familiar. Dee science of economics was still an imperfect one and they would not find a single professor who did not admit that. In its fundamental principles, however, it had had so much examination that they could say that those principles were practicelly settled. There was a lot to do in the investigations of the minor details and it did not seem to him that the field of investigation would become narrower because it meant dealing with the most highly organised being—man. Our wants were far greater than those of our grand- fathers and all those wants came within the sphere of the economiet. Why should they, as young Liberals, study the question ? As young Liberals they had time in front of them and he would impress upon them the necessity, at the outset of their political career, of getting a good acquaintance witu the science of economics. If they only got a smattering of it, it would do good and they would be better able to cope with the questions which must come before the people of the country. The politics of this country at the present time—and they would be in the future—were becoming more and more concerned with social ques- tions. That was a state of affairs which was absolutely necessary. 'I hey all knew the

===== 0002601_19130208_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
WORLD WAS AN IMPERFECT ONE

and they would like to do their little share in trying to improve it. That, he thought, was one of the fundamental ideas underlying the Junior Liberal movement. They all believed that the conditions of the people of our country were of infinitely more impo:rtai.ice to them than any academic discussions. Continuing, the lecturer said that in their study of economics they would find the question of distribution, the most controversial one. He meant by distribution, the way in which the product of industry was shared out heiwen the people who took part in it—to the land- lord as rent, tne capitalist as interest, [tad the labourer as wages. The thorniest part of economics was undoubtedly the money part of exchange. They could however, study economics without going into abstruse questions, and if they got a working know- ledge of the science they would secure benefits far out of proportion to the time spent in obtaining that knowledge. Regarding its application to present-thy problee s, the Chairman had mentioned about Free Trade and 'tariff Reform. He had looked and asked in all quarters for an economic statement of the case for Protection, but had failed to get one— (hear, hear)—whereas the full theory of international trade fit in beautifully with our principles of Free Trade. The question of Tariff Reform was simply a hash of statistics which were not very reliable, and if they ever had any tendency to put faith in those statistics they should take " Punch's advice to those about to marry—Don't. (Laughter.) In the use of statistics the amateur economist should be very careful. Any student of economics could see that they could not make the foreigner pay a toll. There were cases where the foreigner could be made to pay, but they were so exceptional as to be negligible. (Hear, hear). If he was looking at the Insurance Act from an economist's point of view he would say, obviously that Act was intended for securing the increased health of the com- muuity. Such an increase in the health of the com- munity must result in the increased efliciency of the worker. (Hear, hear.) If there was an increased efficiency there must be increased productivity. If there was increased productivity there was room for increased wages. As to whether that increase would come about, depended on other conditions. Speaking scientifically the Insurance Act was an, economical advantage to the country. He believed in labour unrest and he personally thought that if the worker, as the producer, did not get a proportional share of the produce of his industry, he ought to get it. He was sere that part of the increased share would go to give the workman more comforts and the little luxuries which he desired. If they would get hold of the elemen- tary facts of economics they would he in a safe position to examine the cause which underlay the labour unrest—the land question, &c. He had studied economics and it made him enter the arena of politics because that was the only way in which the theories he had, could be applied. By the study of economics they would be able to reason out their political convictions in a scientific manner. (Applause). Mr P 11 RitOwN proposed a hearty vote of thanks to the speaker and said that the great inequality which existed amongst the masses of this country—those who had got wealth and wanted more and those who had not even adequate means—was at the bottom of all our social evils. Mr JoNRS seconded and the resolution was unanimously carried.

ACID STOMACHS ARE

DANGEROUS.

COMMON SENSE ADVICE BY A DISNNGUISEIED SPECIALIST.

" Acid " stomachs are dangerous because acid irritates and in flames the delicate lining of the stomach, thus hindering and preventing the proper action of the stomach, and leading to ninetenths of the cases of stomach trouble from which people suffer. Medicines and medicinal treatments are useless in such cases, for they leave the source of the trouble, the aci d in thc stomach, as dangerous as ever. The acid must be neutralised, and its formation prevented, and the best thing for this purpose is half a teaspoon- ful of bisurated magnesia, a simple antacid, taken iu a little warm or cold water after eating, w Lich not only neutralises the acid, but also prevents the fermentation from which acidity is developed. Foods which ordinarily cause greatest distress can be eaten with impunity if the rceal is followed with a little bisurated magnesia, which can be obtained of ...any chemist, and sLould always be kept handy.

Gordon Dawson's Ordination

We are delighted (says St Paul's Parish Magazine) t° state that one of our old boys, who received his earlY education in our schools, has been ordained by the Lord Bishop of Liverpool. After baing trained as a daY school teacher, Mr Cior,4on Dawson decided to take holy orders. He therefore went to Cambridge, where he had a most brilliant and successful career, and at the ordination examination he came out first, and was there- fore gospeller. His first curacy is at Holy Trinity, Walton Brock. We anticipate a most useful future, aud we w,sh him every success and prusperity. -

Bewsey-road Guild Festival. Address by the Rev. E. B. Keeling.

The annual guild festival in connection with the Bevvsey-road Wesleyan Church was held last week end, h.rge congregations assemoling at each service. The Rev E B Keeling, a former superintendent of the Wesleyan Circuit in Warrington, was the preacher, his addresses being full of inspiration. At tee morning service on Sunday the anthem " From Thy love as a lather," was rendered in a very expressive manner by the choir who were also responsible for a sympathetic rendition of "0 God us Thou appearest," at the even- ing service. At a musical service in the afternoon Dr Bennett of Padgzite presided. The vocalists who took part were Messrs Walker, C Sinclair, Senior, and the Misses Winstanley, V Jones, ‘Vardle and Jones, Mr Winstanley was at the organ. " God's call and the worba's challenge," was the subject of the Rev E B Keeling's lecture to a large assembly on Monday even- ing when Mr J 19 Hayward presid d,and remarked upon his 40 years connection with friends from Bewsey-road and Silver-street school. He wanted the members of, the Guild to be reflectors of Divine influence cud con- vey it on to others. They should be well polished and take the teichiugs they got to heart because if they were not well polished they could not be reflectors. In his lecture Mr KEELINCr said chit on the one side they had God's insistent call to them and the great task He had set before them, and on the ocher they were not persecuted, and were never likely to be. The world regarded them with a certain contemptuous tolerance as amiable enthusiasts who spent a lot of time and i money in certain things n which they were interested. There were tremendous tasks set before them. They were not simply call!ed. to become expert critics of various methods of Ctir!sttan work. There was a great deal of that in the religious papers of to-day. He did not care about the methods so long as the work was done. They should not waste their time denouncing other people. God did not call any church to be infallible and to set up to put all the others right. They should not follow anyone who told them to go back. They were not here to go back but to go forward. (Applause.) They could not get a neat little phrase or set of phrases that were going to save the world. They coull not save the world by administering to it a sort of spiritual pill. (Laughter.) They were called first as Christians, and if they were Christians there should be more of Christ in them than anything else. In the planning of their lives and the arranging of their work, and in the plans they formed Christ ought to have the first pl,ce. They were called to 1)3 real, straight, honest and lendable. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19130208_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
Golborne-street Baptist Church.

Preaching at the above Church on Sunday the Rev 11 Ogden declared that the social outlook was bright and rosy. People were not attending church any better in 1913 than 1912. There were declines in Church attend- ance and Sunday school attendance which the church must take steps to remedy, licit happily the progress of God's kingdom was not commensurate with pews or pulpits, superintedent's, bells and Sunslay school. One of the most hopeful signs was the moral and Spiritual activity beyond the borders of organised religion. A healthy sign was the recognition of woman's equality and rights and were it not for the folly of militant feminism a Bill ere now would have been an Act. Woman is ceasing to be a household drudge and claiming to be what she ought to be, the companion of man. An Englishman who believes that a woman ought to be a household dolt has been born hi the wrong country. He slearld have seen light in Turkey. The emphatic opinion of all England upon the White Slave traffic has manifested the dynamic of woman's new found power. The women who are the horrible sufferers in the tragedy have compelled action. He found himself in total and absolute disagreement with Dr Mellor in his horror of the Act. A brute who will sell a girl as a farmer sells a pig is a brutal coward. 1e dreads nothing but physical punishment. A woman's torture of mind and body are his sport, but when the penalty is writhing physical pain (he knows no mental pain) he flees from it as from a plague. There is little fear of a reversion to a brutal policy for all crimes hut this type most be exterminated from this land; and the honour of rousing England must be credited to the woman who is working to make England cleaner, purer and soberer. That is an evidence we are not getting nearer a second deluge and a Noah's Ark but further from it. There is increase of hope at home. The Insuranoe Act is born from the Sermon on the Mount ; Lloyd George has put the Gospel in an Act of Parliament. Everybody agreed it was the Gospel ; blessed the garment and then pro- ceeded to marry the prize We swagger.about being an imperial race but we left the mother to • face death with platitudes about the grandeur of motherhood. Now the richest Empire the world has ever known allows 303 nivernitv. Alinifl3ent ! But it is a beginning. Everybbdy has sympathized with the con- sumptive but left them with back to back houses, under the shadow of a frowning mill and an atmosphere that could be cut. Now the consumptive has hope. When the out-worker watches the snow flakes falling and rill the land is in the iron grip of winter, the children shall not suffer. Only the bairns of a painter or plasterer realize what it has meant when father has had three months of winter unemployment. Sickness now is handcuffed. The thrifty mother will no longer try every medicine in her own and her sister's cupboard ore sending for the man who knows. The bogey of a d,ctor's bill is laid. Are not such evidences full of hope. Malign the man who has wrought it with all the blackguard tongues an ignorant race can collect, but it is the Gospel in practice. The broad earth is beginning to come back for those for whom it was intended. God is not only in heaven, but He is upon earth. It is well with the world.

DEATH OF MR. BISBROWN, RUNCORN.

The death has cccurred at Runcorn, of Mr Daniel Bisbrown, an ex-chairman of the Runcorn Urban Council and a member of that authority for many years. The deceased gentleman, who had been ailing for many months, was best known as a temperance wcrker. A life-long abstainer, he had been associated with the local SODS of Temperance for over thirty years, and was a trustee of the Cheshire grand Division.

UNSAFE GRID.

Singular Accident Ends in County Court Claim.

£9O Damages. The sequel to an unusual accident was heard at the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Stanger, when Miss Jane Lamb Woods, dressmaker and costumier, Rylands-street, suedi John Richard Evans, solicitor, and H B Turner,railliner and draper, the owner and tenant respectively of 17, Palmyra-square,:for £lOO damages for injuries sustained and loss of business incurred by falling down a cellar its consequence of the insecure and dangerous condition of the coal plate which opene:j r orn the footpath. Mr W B Forshaw appeared for the plaintiff and Mr A Browne represented the defendants. The original claim was for £l3O but it was reduced to £lOO to bring it within the jurisdiction of the County Court. Defendants had paid into Court £75 damages and £7 73 costs.

-The case for the plaintiff was that she was going from business to her home on June 11th and when pass- ing along Palmyra-square she stepped on to tile coal plate in question, which gave way, letting her down into the aperture. She bruised both her legs very severely ana cut one of them. She managed to get home and Dr Fox was sent for and bandaged the wounds. Plaintiff was in bed for several weeks and was away from business for thirteen weeks. The accident happened at the busiest stage of Miss Wood's season, as it was only a few weeks before Walking Day, which was Warrington's great festival. Plaintiff made an effort to resume work a few days after the accident, but had to take to her bed again. Erysipelas set in and for several days her condition was so grave that the doctor was quite uncertain as to her ultimate recovery. She vomited repeatedly and was delirious. At the present time she was not completely recovered from the effects of her illness and suffered from exhaustion when she had to be on her feet for any L length of time. Mr Forshaw submitted that the nelig- en ce of which the defendants had been guilty was not of an ordinary kind, but of the grossest possible type. Only a fortnight--

His Honour- interposed with the comment that, as liability for the accident was not denied by the defend- ants, the degree of negligence did not concern him. He simply had to consider the consequences which ordinarily flowed from the accident and had not to award more damages because the negligence was grave. Mr Forshaw contended that the original claim for £l3O was exceedingly reasonable. It was made up as follows :—Loss of business (thirteen weeks), £39 : medical attendance, £5 5a ; nursing (thirteen weeks), £27 63 ; medical appliances &c., £8 ; pain and suffering £5O. Mr Forshaw explained that Miss Woods was in partnership with her sister but her earnings might he estimated at quite £3 a week. During her illness extra work had to be done by her sister and even then it was impossible to cope with all the business.

The plaintiff was called and cross-examined as to her share of the profits of the business, admitted that neither she nor the firm paid income tax, exemption having been successfully claimed. Mr Browne: 1-lave you had anything deducted from your share of the profits, in consequence of your illness? Plaintiff : Well, I shall be short, certainly. How much will you be short ?—I could not tell you. Further evidence in support of the claim was given by Dr Fox, cud tho Misses Lucy and Edith Woods. Mr Browne said his clients never attempted to deny liability and they had tried, in all ways possible, to effect an amicable settlement. There had been uo• documents produced to give an exact idea of the loss sustained. The whole thing was mere surmise, the only figures produced showing a decrease of only £lO in the gross profits for four months, as compared with the previous year. Defendants admitted liability for such damages as the plaintiff could prove she had sustained. One would have thought that, if the claim had been real aud not exaggerated, the plaintiff would have been only too pleased to avoid coming into court by producing the necessary documents and books. She had not done so, but had pursued a " dog in the maneer " policy. It had been a case of" £lOO and not a half-penny less." Defendants, he submitted, had acted handsomely in paying £75 into Court. That allowed for n trade loss of £25, which was more than generous. To ask £5O for pain and suffering was altogether unreasonable.

His Honour, in giving judgment, sgirl he did nt see any indicatiou of a desire to inflate the damages. Juries, however, did not generally award damages for pain and suffering on anything like the scale claimed by the plaintiff. Many people might say "I would not undergo that pin for any amount of money," but that, was not the standard to be applied. They had to con- sider what was reasonable compensation. As to the loss of business, the materials before him were some- what scanty, but when they considered that the plaintiff did skilled work, her absence from business tor thirteen weeks must have entailed a substantial loss. However, he did not think the fall amount of the claim had been proved. His Honour awarded damages to the amount of £9O.

ON THE 'PHONE.

" Hello ! That the Exchange? Give me 'Suits Limited,' please. . . . the number? I don't know, its in Horsemarket street—Jubilee Buildings. (Wbur-mr-r)

HOCKEY.

LATCH FORD. Latchford won a keen game at St. Helens on a heavy ground last Saturday by 2-1, this being the fifth succes- sive victory. Simp•on netted for the home side and Pearson for the visitors in the first half, the latter player also hitting the crossbar with a splendid shot. On changing round Latchford pressed heavily and hot shots from Minards, Starkey and Pearson were cleverly kept out by Mercer, the St Helens goalkeeper, who was in great form, but at length Pearson eluded the backs and scored a winning goal which gave Mercer no chance Clark, right back, Green, centre half, and Rudd, outside right, were the best of the St Helens team, and for Latchford, Wilkinson was the best uf the oacks, and Dale and Pearson of the forwards. The Second Elevens met at Latchford and the v!sitors were beaten by 7-3, but they made a much better show than they usually do against Latchfor:i and at one time were leading. Turner, their right back, put up a strong defence and was difficult to pass. Latchford had the help of E Cliffe at inside-right and he played a rasp- ing game and shot four of the goals. Fletcher (1) and Isherwood (2) scoring the others. Gibbs at centre half played a hard game and kept the St Helms forwards down.

"Warrington's only home match this month takes ,place to-day, and granted decent weather, spectators .can be relied upon to roll up in large numbers to welcome Widnes. The " Chemicals " have done themselves rather well at Warrington's expense during the last few seasons, but the worm should have a turn to-day. Widnes have fallen away from a rather com- manoing position for a local side, and Warrington have "come on" a bit. The Wirepullers should certainly regain the lost 50 per cent. to-day. The corresponding match a season ago resulted in a draw Of 5 points each.

Only a couple of years ago it appeared as if "Jack" Smith and his Committee bad solved the problem of /raining up a purely local team which could hold its own with the best. The Widnesians were then high in the League table, and even went so far as to conquer Wigan at Wigan and Warrington at Wilders- pool. With a comparatively young team, the prospect seemed decidedly rosy, and it is not to the best interests of the game that I those bright anticipations had been dimmed. The rise of Widnes has not been maintained, and to-day they are not to be ranked above the ordinary second-raters (a class which unfortunately includes Warrington). Widnes have still a good local team, but it is not equal to the best, and a long row needs ho.3ing before any such claim can be made for the plucky "Chemicals." But they will go on trying, and they have the best wishes of all sports- men who see that the future of the N.U. game lies With the home grown lads of Lancashire and Yorkshire, and nut with the Colonial and other " impJrts."

Most clubs are doing their best to grow tke natural footballer, and Warrington among them. But it takes time. Meanwhile gaps must and will be filled from fields afar by clubs who can afford to pay for the qualified player. The Warrington team to-clay Will contain seven "local lads," and with Brooks and B. radshaw fit again, nine out of thirteen will be Indigenous to the soil, so to speak. But it must not be forgotten that we owe a good deal, all the same, to the men from \Vales and elsewhere who have done yeoman service in the primrose and blue jersey.

The team to-day is the same as last week's, viz. : Jolley, full back ; Williams, Renwick, Tranter and Davies, three-quarters ; Nicholas and Daintith, half- backs; G Thomas, R Thomas, Cox, Chesters, Pearnley and Skolhorne, forwards.

The Widnes team will be: Brassington, full back; Reid, Taylor, Redmond, J O'Gara, three-quarters; Noon and J Lally, half backs; Aspey, Uorcoran, Cosgrove, Higgins, A O'Gara and Lloyd, forwards. Refere, Mr Renton (lunslet).

Wart ington lost at Leeds, but they again made a creditable show. The Leeds forwards had laid them- selves out to avenge the " gruelling " sustained at Wilderspool, and it must be admitted that they got the better of the wiry Warringtonians. In the loose, Jack was as good as his master, but in the scrummage Work Leeds had a big pull and obtained possession of the ball much more frequently than the visitors. Pphind they were speedier, and the two things corn- blued proved too much for the sturdy Warrington 'defence. It is speed among the backs that we lack roost, and until we get it the scoring power of the team litst necessarily remain below par.

--- h The Leeds ground was about the worst Warrington d::43 played on this season, the mud being fully ankle 13- Considering the conditions the game was fetearltably fast and clever. Both sides played good :°tball, and few better contested games have been iielert between the two clubs. Warrington scored first rough Tranter's opp rtunism, hut Leeds gradually °l.ged ahead and won 143. It was about a fair reflex of the sawe.

On the Warrington side no player gained greater thonour than Jolley, who defended with great skill and .pluck. He had a busy afternoon dealing with the dashing raids of the home forwards, but he was never .once found wanting. His tackling was remarkable for a player of such modest physique. At half back, too, Nicholas played a sterling game, though twice 'knocked out by the aggressive Leeds forwards. The Warrington threequarter line did not make a great -show on the attack, but they defended well, Widianis tackling splendidly.

Three league games were played on Tues3ay. Widnes were heavily defeated by 26 points to 5 at Dewsbury. Wigan won at Halifax IS-7, and Hull at I.loel4dale 11-0.

Warrington at Leeds.

Warrington took a strong team to Leeds on Satur- day, to play the return fixture with the Headiugley club. Having been well beaten at Warrington, the homesters were keen on righting matters and after it very keen game they sent the Wirepallers hack defeated The weather was unsettled, but fully 7,000 assembled. The ground was in a shocking state and it was remark- able how the teams contrived to play such good foot- ball in a sea of mud. Leeds introduced Stacey, the smart Keighley wing into their team, but he was unable to take a hand in the scoring. The teams were: Warrington : back ; Williams, Tranter Renwick and Davies, three quarter-backs ; Dainteth and Nicholas, half-hacks ; G Thomas, Chesters, Skelhorne, Fearnley, Cox and R Thomas, ferwards. Leeds : Haycock, back ; Davies, Canip'ell, Sullivan ' and Stacey, threequarter-backs ; Senders and Gatiley, half-backs ; Webster, Harrison, Milli:4d, Evans, Ward and Sutton, forwerds. Referee, Mr A Hestferd (Broughton Rangers), touch- judges, Messrs E Willoughoy (Bradford) fuel W H Garside (Halifax). The Leeds men opened in confilent style, and a brilliant plece of handling with the dry ball almost brought about Warrington's downfall in the first few minutes. Campbell was checked close in by Jolley, who made a great tackle. Warrington were kept busily employed on defence, and some floe repelling work was done by Tranter, Renwick and Jolley, before the War- rington forwards found their feet and carried play to the other end. Skelhorae and Cheaters were prominent in the loose, and soon had the ball hovering near the Leeds line. Harrison and Ward got away with the hall at their toes, but were soon sent back and Warrington attacked with spirit. Sanders attempted to pass near his own line, with the result that the watchful Tranter snapped up the ball and romped over the Leeds line. Jolley failed at goal. Faced with this reverse, Leeds attacked hotly, but without avail for some time. Leeds eventually scored, Sanders running over. Haycox failed at the goal-kick. \Vith the teams equal, the game was finely contested by both sides. Davies scored for Leeds. Half-time: Leedsesix points; ‘Varrington, three points: The second period was brimful of interest. There were moments when Warrington failed and gave the home men a taste of their quality, but for the most part the home team were the masters of the situation. Campbell, Sullivan and Davies continued to exhibit good form, and Stacey was often alert in taking his passes. but over-eagerness on his part proved his undoing. Campbell and Mirfield succeeded in penetrat lug the Warrington defence, both tries being the result of sound and accurate football. Result : Leed,, one goal four tries points): Warrington, one try (three points).

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORI:INGNIEN'S ANGLING ASSOCIATION. (N.A. CENTRE)

This last week or so the conditions for angling have been rather bad. What with the rain and floods and rapid running streams, the angler has had little chance v‘ith the fish. The Gowy and Shropshire Union Canal have both been tried with poor results and we are now only waiting for better conditions to make a start on the set board. The membership at headquarters is improving weekly. A batch of 24 names was handed to the secretary last week-end. The Bay Horse will have a membership of ZO or over and the sets and specials will entice many more. The Cooper's Arms are also booking up members. Hero again is the making of a good society ; they bave several old hands at the helm to steer them through the season.

===== 0002601_19130208_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GOW Y.

The few members who visited the Gowy met with poor results. There are times when we are crying out for a little colour in the water but at present we have had more than enough. It has quite upset all calcula- tions, and I know halts of every description have been used and the fish are still masters of the situation both at Little Barrow and the lower reaches by the Shaker Bridge. Angling is prohibited from Mr Hassall's land as is also the privilege of walking over the land to get to the deep holes by the Shaker Bridge. Those who wish to fish this part had better take the route by what is known as the " dirty lane," otherwise they will [lace to travel round by Stanney Brook if they fish co the near side to Chester and want to get down to the bottom end. This means about tbreequarters of an hour's walk,and one must know his way about to do it in that time. Of course Stepney Brook can be fished on the way down. Here some fine specimen dace have their haunts hut it does not seem to be popular like the lower reaches. Mr Hassell is to put up sign boards to warn anglers to keep off his meadows and we advise all members not to approach the fisheries in that direction, as the society will not be held responsible for them after this warning and ii, would save considerable inconvenience to the officials of the society if they take this warning in time. We hear it will only he closed for twelve months and anglers can rest assured that they are not the cause of it being closed this season. Mr Hassell has his own private reasons and he distinctly says that he has always found the angler only too willing to assist him in all matters, besides having several friends among them.

THE COOPERS ARMS SOCIETY.

A general meeting was held on Monday last, a good muster of members putting in appearance. Air F Trigger took the chair at 8 o'clock and a good night's business was gone through. Their was a slight altera- tion in the committee, Mr H Owen being elected in place of Mr Holloway. After a discussion of about flftsen minutes it was finally decided that all membErs of this branch join the club at headquarters. Thus members here are fully qualified to weigh for the centre club's match plizes and set prizes ; it will also save them going to the expense of buying contribution cards &c. They have collected a fine lot of prizes for the first year, which should entice mary anglers to throw in their lot with them. The specials collected are as follows :—Grayling 2s 63, roach 2s 6d and 25 Gd. dace 2s lid, daddy rtiffe 2s 6d, gudgeon 2s lid, smallest flounder 2s Gd, bream 2s Gd and 2s 6d, trout 2s Gd, perch 2s Gd and 2s Gd, pike 2s 6d, eel 2s 6d, chub 2s 6d, making a total of £1 178 Gd, not a bad lot of specials to be going on with, and their secretary informs me there are more to follow. A band of anglers of this description is bound to have a successful sesson, because each member throws in his lot for the club's welfare, and I sincerely trust it will not be long before I am reporting more progress through the 'Examiner."

FISHING IN NEW ZEALAND.

According to reports to hand from New Zealand, which is generally recognised by anglers as being the finest country in the world for trout fishing, this seison promises to provide splendid sport for the followers of Izaac Walton. " The °tura River is full of trout " states Mr C Wal'nut, of the Government Tourist Department, the local acclimatisation society having been steadily stocking the river with yearling trout for some years, with the result that it now contains trout rp to 30 inches in length. Similar reports come from Tearoha and liakahi, and the Waikato River is said to be yielding better sport then ever, whi-Ar is saying a great deal. Since the opening of the season at the Lake Taupo splendid fishing has been obtained. The fish are in excellent condition and a record season is anticipated. One party recently took 107 fish weighing 634 lbs in four nays. The most successful party, however, was three gentlemen, who in three days secured 116 fish weighing 7421b5, a record for the lake.

Mr J A Carney, fishing at the north side of the Rangitata mouth on Thursday night and Friday morn- ing, landed 19 fish, the two largest weighing 71b each. An tinuscal feature of the catch was the landing of two fish from one cast, one on minnow. weighing 51bs, and the other on a fly, weighing 3Dhs. Mr Carney states that the trout are more plentiful this season than for the last six years.

"I think the growth of our modern industrial system INas more to do with the indifference of people to churches, than anything else," said the Rev. F. Hibbert, of Southpurt (and formerly of Blackburn) at a special service held under the auspices of the War- rington and District Free Churcn Council, in the, Friars Green Independent Methodist Church, on Thurs- day evening. A well-attended service was conducted in the afternoon and in the evening there was another fair attendance when the principal speakers were the Rev J Gwilym Jones (St. Helens), Rev Fred Hibbert (Southport), and Mr C E Parker, J.P. The Rev A Harvey, of Widnes, preided at the outset of the meet- ing, in the absence of Mr Parker, who did not return from London until the first seeaker had addressed the congregation. " One of the special tasks lying ahead of us,is that of organising the Empire," was one of the Rev J G Joss' opening sentences. It was not the first time in the march of history that the task of organising an Empire had devolved upon the people. In the first century of our Christian era that was precisely the task which confronted the citizens of Rome. The problem they had to solve was 'how to bind together into one grand and harmonious whole, the far-sundered lands and many diverse peeples over which they held sway." That was their problem end it was ours to-day, only the problem which confronted us was a more complex and more urgent one. We were not merely concerned with the externals of the problem but with its inner implications and issues. Our task was not merely to organise an Empire, but to organise and create a society—which should not only connote a common citizenship but a common brotherhood. Our deepest problems were not those which separated East from West, or White ' from Black, but those which separated man from man, clan from clan, and creed from creed. Ours was not merely ti. political problem, it was an economic and social and a relieious problem. How were we to solve it ? Could the first century help us here. He must confess that he was profoundly impressed by the way in which the statesmen of ancient Rome sought to solve the pressing problems of their day. It had,he was quite sure, meaning and significance for us. They wanted to weld the scattered units of their vast Empire into one visible and tangible whole. They took the person of their Emperor and deified his person and made of him a God. They sot him upon a sacred pedestal and ordained that all their subject peoples should worship him. He was not concerned with the ethical or with the religious aspect of this solution. He knew how it _ _ SHOOK TO THE VERY FOUNDATIONS. _

r the early Christian communities. To bend the knee to Clesar was impossible for those who bent the knee to Christ. He wanted them to note that those Roman statesmen in search of a unifying consolidating principle, found it in the appeal to the religious sentiment in man, in the appeal to the religious faculty in human nature. All the subjects . became one in the worship of Cesar. The significance 'of that had a meaning for us of this 20th century. We too were in search of a unifying consolidating principle. We were weary of our divisions, of our estrangements, and our antagonisms. We were weary of the deep fissures and the broad yawning gulfs of our modern world. We were seeking some principle of cohesion, some principle of solidarity, some magic power which would enable us to bridge our ugly differences and to bury our ugly enmities. We must look in the same direction as did the patriots and politicians of Rome. We, too, must make our appeal to the religious senti- ment in man and the religious faculty in human nature, only witti the vital difference that where they had put CLesar we must put Christ, and where they put the Empire we must put the whole world. He was pro- foundly persuaded that the bottom need of our world to-day was a vision of the mystical transcendental, kingly Christ. The bottom need of the world to-day was to see Him high over all, yet in all. Where no vision was the people perished. The waggon tarried , that was not hitched to a star, and the waggon of our modern and multi-complex civilization was tarrying because it needed to be hitched to the Star of Bethlehem.

A stirring and inspiring address was given by the Rev Fred Hibbert who said they heard a great del to-day of the decadence of churches and they all lamented that people did not attend church as they should do. (Hear, hear.) There was one point about it arid that was that by looking too much after those who did not go to church they were apt to over look those who did go. There was no disputing the fact that our churches were not holding their own as they would like them to do. Alongside of that they had the fact that the spirit of Christ was more powerful and more operative to-day than ever it had been during all the Christian centuries. The Christian spirit was making itself manifest in every department of life in a way which it had never done before. (Hear, hear.) , Alongside that, they had the decadenca of the power of the Church. There were a number of reasons which could be given to explain in some degree the indiffer- ence of the people to the Church and the decadence of Churche's power. For example, there was the growth of the scientific spirit in our day which had taken away a great deal of the authority and poser or the preacher, and the authority and power of the Church. People had learned through the growth of the scientific spirit to inquire for themselves and to ask for a reason why things are, and why things are said to be. Thirty five years ago the authority and power of the preacher was very different to what it was to day. Personally, he thought it was a very good thing that the power of the minister had gone—he meant in the way it used to be regarded. The spirit of criticism and inquiry which had made a man inquire and ask questions had also come into the pulpit. For a long time now, the pulpit had not been speaking with the authority it used to do. If a man showed that he had the courage of his own convictions there he would be listened to. They had been preaching a great deal of "perhaps " which was in no way desirable. He also thought the growth of our modern industrial system had had more to do with the indifference of people to church than anything else. The application of steam to productive industries bad made this country of ours increase in wealth beyond all dreams of avarice. The result was that the individuals had got wealthier. Wealth bad increased by leaps and bounds and

MILLIONAIRES WERE No \V BECOMING COMMON. A time of prosperity was always a time of great common danger. Great wealth produced great luxury, and greet luxury resulted in atheism ; not the atheism of not believing there was a God, but the atheism that left God and religion out of the count. They found now, that Sunday was becoming a day of entertainment for the wealthy people. They played golf, tennis, and went motoring. The top end of the scale, through wealth and luxury, developed a spirit of indifference which got them out of touch with the churches. They had come to feel the power of money, but they had lest the feel of the spiritual things which they could not see. (Hear, hear.) At the lower end of the scale he believed the condition of the ordinary people was neves better than to-day. There had seen a great upward movement. He was also sure there never was a time when the pressure of life on a great many people was so hard as it was to-day. Within this country with all its wealth they hail millions of people living on the border line of poverty, and a good number below it. When they considered the enormous pressure of life and the struggle there was to keep body and soul together they could understand when he said that numbers of people had neither the time or the inclination to con- sider the spiritual life. Work was also becoming less interesting. (Hear, hear.) Through the growth of competition and the development of industries it was becoming necesssry to specialise in processes of pro- duction. Work, through the process of specialisation was becoming monotonous and uninteresting, with the result that men must have some other outlook to stimulate interest. They could not spend all their lives without something interesting and exciting, hence,they had the enormous rage for pleasure and tremendous crowds of people going to see football matches. 'I hose things were the direct result of our industrial system tending in the directions in which it had gone. Those, to him, were some simple reasons why the churches were not holding, their own end not keeping in close touch with the minds and hearts of the pevle. Such a state of affairs was calling them to action in certain directions. He believed there never was a time in the history of the world when the work of the Christian Church was needed more so than to-day. (Ilear, hear.) And there was nothing in our society to-day that could take the place of the Christian Ceurch as the manufactory of Christian character. (Hear hear.) They were not Christians because they belonged to a church; they belonged to the church because they were Christians. They also took sacrament because they were Christians, and were not Chriqi..ns ho3Aus3 they took sacrament, The Free Church Council movement arose out of the realisation of that truth that religion was separate from everything else. They did not want united churches but a united Christendom, all of them being loyal to their own churches and realising and feeling that although they marched in different regiments they all made one great army and were loyal to the one Captain. One of the great obstacles to progress of the Church was the ecclesiastical and denominational differences which

were so prominent. They should bring the churches together in one fellowship, brotherhood and federation that God's Kingdom might come on earth as it is in Heaven. (Applause). Mr C E PARKER. said ho did not think

.

interested in the Free Church movement. He recognised its weakness and its limitations brit he thought it had done a great work in creating a better spirit amongst them. (Hear, hear.) There was the spirit of unity amongst them, and it was only in so far as they realised the great importance of the central truth of Christ's mission that the other things shrank into comparative insignificance, and out of that spirit would come the closer union of the churches. He trusted they %%mild throw their hearts into the Free Church work in Warrington, because it stood for a great deal in regard to the consolidation and eff 3ctive influence of Free Churches in Warrington and the district.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"Kit Carson."

The two essentials of a good drama—a large amcunt of sensationalism and enough humour to relieve the heavier aspects, to a certain degree—are found in Dottridge, Longden and Liell's presentation of the popular drama " Kit Carson, the blind detective," which has attracted large audiences to the Royal Court Theatre during the week. It is a capital story of love, hatred, and revenge, incidentally depicting life in the under-world. In the title role Mr John Millward is highly successful, his acting being power- ful yet polished. Miss Georgette Thierry shares equally the honours in her capital delineation of Norah Patterson, the widow. She infuses real life into the part and is very successful in the scene where she is confronted by her husband whom she suppoi;ed was dead. Mr Charles If Longden is the typical "bruiser" in the character of Ned Patterson, the revengeful, escaped convict. Mr Edward Vivian is a clever actor and as Mervyn Fitzroy, the swell thief soon earns the execrations of the audience. It is be who is pitted ! against the detective and some exciting situations are witnessed. Miss Marie Loni,den solicits with success the sympathies of the audience as Jack Patterson, the happy-go-lucky schoolboy. Mr Henry Deane as Jim Poole. is a useful foil in the hands of Fitzroy and he plays the part in a telling mancer. Miss Cathleen Cavanagh makes him a capital partner in her repre- sentation of the difficult character of" Deaf Annie. ' The other characters are well sustained and the stag- ing of the drama is excellently carried out..

ECZEMA ON NECK AND HEAD.

NATIONAL PROVINCIAL BANK OF . ENGLAND, LIMITED.

The annual general meeting of this hank was held on Thursday at 15, Bishopsgate, E.C. Mr M 0 Fitzgerald, who presided, said that no one could maintain that the year 1912 had been dull or devoid of feature. Here at home we had bud constitutional and social legislation of the first importance,vvhile trade had been distracted by two prolonged labour strikes. Abroad the Turco- Italian War was only brought to an end in order that one of the combatants—Turkey—might engage in a far more doiperate struggle with her neighbours in Europe. In the Far East, too, the unsettled state of China he, given cause for much anxiety. In spite of the, (118- turbing obstables, the tide of the world's cummerce, reinforced no doubt by the fine harvests in Eastern Europe and in North and South America, had continued to flow in increasing volume, and had indeed risen to an unprecedented height. With regard to their own business, naturally the activity of trade had caused a persistent demand for credit, and rates for loans and discounts bad been well maintained through- the year. In the second six months the war between Turkey and the Balkan Allies brought about pro- nounced stringency in more than one European centre, and it remained to be seen bow far, when the moratorium in Central Europe expired, creditors in those countries would be able to meet their liabilities. The war expenditure had caused a great wastage of capital ; there were large floating debts await- ing a favourable opportunity to find a per- manent home ; trade continued abnormally active, and the more backward countries were being developed with increasing enterprise. All these indications pointed to a vei y large and sustained demand for capital, and after making allowance for some hoarding on the Continent owing to fear of war, there were not wanting signs that the demand was over- taking the supply. On the other hand, the supply which was maintained to a large extent by the savings of the thrifty, was being seriously cut into by the heavy and ever-increasing taxation and the unproductive expenditure of almost all Governments. In our ovsn case about is lid of every £ of taxes was expended on the naval and military services. This demand for capital had naturally enhanced its value, and the higher rates which had obtained for money were reflected in the figures which the directors had the pleasure to present that day. The profits of the Bank showed an increase of £57,000; the deposits had also increased during the year by £2,700,000, and the advances by £2,000,000. rho disheartening feature again was the continued depreciation of gilt-edged securities, obliging them to provide a sum of £199,000. There was one small crumb of comfort in this continual writing down, and that was that it increased the rate of interest earned by the securities so written down. This increase of rates now amounted to something tangible and would be a distinct benefit in years to come especially should there be a prolonged period of cheap money. To the future they could look forward with confidence. Their new branches were almost without exception giving good promise, and the bank was man excellent position to do justice to the increasing busines3 which they might confidently lock for. The report vraa enasimeusly adopted.

EARL POWIS'S ESTATE TO BE SOLD.

Lord Powis has decided to dispose privately of the Montford Estate, Shrewsbury, which extends to 5,577 acres.

500,000• TON CONTRACT.

Swedish State railway contracts for coal amounting to 530,000 tons were allotted on the Newcastle-on-Tyne Exchange on Thursday. The price per ton was 14s 9d, and the contracts were divided almost equally between Newcastle and Scotland.

CRISIS IN THE OPIUM TRAFFIC. FORCED ON CHINA BY THE BAYONET.

" This public me-ting believing that the growth and manufacture of opium in India tor export to China is a grievous wrong and a serious hindrance to friendly relations with that Empire, urges upon his Majesty's Government that without delay China be formally released from a reaty obligations to admit Indian opium, and that the connection of the Indian Govern- ment with tbe opium trade for other than medical pur- poses be brought to an end."—Such was the resolution passed unanimously by a large assembly in the Friends' Meeting House, Buttermarket-street, on Tuesday evening. Mr Noel Broomhall (of the Anti-Opium League) gave an interesting lecture illustrated by limelight views, on the subject of the opium curse, and it was on his proposition that the resolution was passed The Rev W CoorEit, who presided, said there were many reasons why they ought to have a meeting of that description. First of all they ought to take some steps in the matter to clear the way for our missionaries in China. They went over there to preach the Gospel and introduce the Bible, yet the people whom they met could rather sarcastically remind them that the very country which had sent them had also imposed the curse of opium upon them. That curse was, to China, as bad as the curse of alcohol was to us in England. (Hear, hear.) For that reason, if for no other, we ought to take some steps in the matter. (Applause). Mr BRoomirALL prefaced his lecture by remarking that he thought it was necessary that someone should go about to inform the people of this country how terribly connected our Ciovernruent was with this matter and how responsible we were as a nation for the opium evil. Many excellent lantern slides were shown depicting the manufacture of opium from the poppy plant to the actual smoking of the drug. Other pictures illustrated the evil effects opium smoking had on its devotees. Opium smoking prevailed not only in China but large quantities of the drug were used in French Cochin China, Burma, and other countries. The population of China was 400,000,000. Surely when we considered what trouble we had with our temper- ance reform in our little island, it should give us great sympathy with China in her gigantic work of opium reform. (Hear. hear.) The anti-opium crusade in this country had done much to rid China of this curse. We had gone to war with China over the opium question and they had been known as opium wars. China had known for 400 years what the evil effects of opium were and she had tried to check it. The duty was fixed at 10 per cent on the value of the drug at first, but a few yearn ago China was allowed to increase it to 20 per cent. We, in this country put 50 per cent and 100 per cent on some of the tea which we imported, yet China was forbidden to put a heavier duty than 20 or 20 per cent on opium. It was high time she was released from the abominable treaty. Opium ruined health and shortened the life of the smoker. Tne question with regard to the opium traffic was one upon which all the churches were agreed„ that it was a sin against God and against man. The Chinese amongst themselves, had anti-opium societies and their endeavour was to persuade the young from starting to smoke the dreaded drug. Archdeacon Woolf° had said "that the devil could not have invented a more pernicious device than opium smoking for ruining life, body and soul." The craving for opium was far more intense than that for alcohol and it was far worse to give it up. If a smoker tried to give it up without medical aid, he ran the risk of losing his life. Children could buy opium in India as easily as children in this country could obtain sweets. Opium pills were given by the mothers of India to quieten their children. It was high time some drastic steps were taken in order to free China from a curse which was ruining her sons and daughters. He proposed the resolution which would be sent to the Right Hon Sir Edward Grey, Foreign Office, Right Hon Earl of Crewe, India Office ; Right Hon H Harcourt, Colonial Office ; Mr Bonar Law and Mr Harold Smith, M.P.

Mr 3 A RANSOME seconded the resolution, and the Rev W COOPER, in supportieg, said we had forced the traffic on China at the point of the bayonet. He trusted that the English conscience would be roused on the matter and that before long the business would be brought to an end. (Applause).
