THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY FEBRUARY 8, 1913.

Although the general • G. 8.5." AND THE position in the fight for SPEAKER. women's suffrage is that of anti-climax following the decease of the Franchise Bill and its amendments, there is none of that apathy abroad which usually follows on the morrow of a crisis which has been as inde- cisive as it 11i been disappointing. Indeed, both the supporters and opponents of votes for women seem to have derived renewed vigour from renewed opportunity, and the question is still, perhaps, the liveliest feature of the politics of the moment. Mr Bernard Shaw, too, has made a characteristic sort of entry into the fray—an entry and no entry- Protesting that he "shall not meddle," and that it "had better be left to the women," " G.B 5.," nevertheless writes to the Queen's Hall meeting to indicate what he should have said had he decided to take the platform. So we have to deal with a speech and no speech, and with an attack on the Speaker which Mr Shaw did not make! " G. 8.5." has no scruples in accusing the Speaker of gross par- tiality in his decision that the women's amendments were out of order, and it is sig- nificant that this is the first noteworthy criticism of the kind which has been made during the fight. Many hold that the Speaker's ruling was not correct. It has been left to Mr Shaw—in a speech which he "should have made "—to allege insincerity. There is no need for us, or for anybody else, to take up the cudgels on behalf of Mr Speaker Lowther. Parliament nor People require no convincing on this matter. Meanwhile, the

various groups in the suffrage camps are pur- suing their diverse courses. Some are con- sidering the Prime Minister's offer for next session, others are demanding a Government measure, and others again are smashing win- dows. What can be the outcome of such a futile pother? Probably it must court further disappointment and defeat. There is plenty of enthusiasm, plenty of activity, and plenty of heat; but the one thing lacking is the elementary condition of success or of appreci- able progress—co-ordination and co-opera- tion. A united, determined, and well-ordered campaign by the combined forces which demand the vote for women would increase public sympathy, and strengthen the hands of a Parliament which prpbablv possesses a potential. majority in favour of women's enfranchisement.

There is reasonable PARR HALL hope that the Corpora- ORGAN. tion will shortly under- take the provision of a new organ in Parr Hall. Though consider- able opposition was manifested at Tuesday's Council meeting, there was a good majority- in favour of a re-consideration of the Sub- Committee's proposals, and the Mayor pledged himself to bring the matter to a head. It is a strong argument that while so much is being done in the way of providing----" facilities for the outdoor recreation of the people, the Council has long been deaf to the legitimate claim of those who look upon indoor music as one of the delights and solaces of a busy and possibly humdrum life. The Corporation owns Parr Hall, and it is not treating tenants of that building fairly to debit them with the use of an organ which is admittedly inefficient and beyond repair.A modern instrument would be a boon to the public and a means of increasing not only the love of music, but the usefulness of the building which Alderman Tinnion unkindly calls a "white elephant." During the debate there was some pertinent criticism of the Musical Society, and the suggestion that it needs democratising is one that merits more than passing notice.

The Town Council still TOWN COUNCIL maintains a policy of AND PROPERTY secrecy in regard to PURCHASING, its rather numerous purchases of property. We can well understand that while negotia- tions are in progress, the fewer people who are "in the know " and the better. But when a price has been agreed upon, then we think that both the Council and the public have a right to the information. There were several items on the private agenda (this in itself a modern innovation) before Tuesday's Council meeting, which related to property purchasing. Prices were stated in print for the information of the Council,but not for the press or the public. Councillors Pemberton and Lash ford attempted to introduce a discussion on one of the bargains, but were ruled out of order by the Mayor after a discreditable scene, which will make amusing reading for the cynic, but which was degrading for any public body. It appears to us that the two CouncillOrs were quite within their rights under Standing Order 42, and we are inclined to question the wisdom of his Worship the Mayor in over-ruling this Order, which specifically' states that "no property shall be purchased by any official or Committee without the sanction of the Council." It is a fact .that the bulk of opinion in the Council was on the side of the Mayor, but it certainly seems that "the sanction of the Council" must be considered a mere empty form of words in regard to purchases of property. If no individual councillor has .the right to question the propriety or price of purchases on the point of being concluded, how is he to fulfil his trusteeship for the ratepayers ?

Time was when the THE LICENSING Warrington Licensing SESSIONS. Sessions were the occasion of mighty con- flicts between temperance reformers and "the trade," but yesterday's proceedings were quite uneventful. It is a cause for satis- faction that the magistrates are steadily pursuing the policy of reducing licences, so , far as the compensation fund will allow. Both justices and police seem to have set their faces against the granting of any new licences and the only application which came before them yesterday—for an off- licence to sell bottled beer on brewery premises—was refused, in the absence of any quid pro quo in the form of surrenders. The report of the Chief Constable, with its tell-tale statistics, does not make pleasant reading for those who wish Warrington to rank as a sober town. IMeetings are being held at various works in the town to encourage the employees to continue or increase their contributions to the Infirmary. Mr. Roger Parr pointed out to Messrs. W. D. Houghton's men•that only one-third of the persons treated at the Infirmary were insured persons. The Insurance Act will, we feel sure, eventually impress the workingmen of the country with the value of medical aid as a public privilege. There will then be no quibbling about giving the help that is so essential to institutions like the Infirmary. *

The knob-sticking of the Tramways Com- mittee in regard to the painting of the electric standards resolves itself, on inquiry, into a vary simple matter. Alderman Smethurst explains that a few standards have been whitewashed "so that people going home late at night would not run against them." After all, the Committee has been considerate rather than inconsiderate. Imagine the plight of the out-late husband who might come home with a bruise.on his cranium, and the excuse that he had collided with a dark and deadly tram-standard. Thanks to the coat of whitewash the delinquents will not get off so easily, and fond wives be less readily deceived.

* - A new, and we hope happy and prosperous era commenced at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday, when the Rev. T, T. James, M.A., of Sheffield, entered upon Ms ministry in the pulpit which has withessed such able and devoted service from the Rev. G. S. Reaney and the Rev. John Yonge. Only seven months have elapsed since Mr. onge's long period of labour at Wycliffe terminated, and the new minister follows in the footsteps of two men who were notable not only in their parochial sphere, but in the life and activities of the community. Mr. James brings youth, enthusiasm, a great capacity for work and faith to aid him in rejuvenating local Con- gregationalism. It has declined some- what with changing environments, but the, future is bright with hope. A new chapel is being built at the Padgate end of the town, and the Mother Church is ripe for development and enterprise in Christian warfare. The Rev. T. T. James has already avowed an intention of enlisting a greater proportion of young people in his work and with the whole-hearted support of the con- gregation we have no fear that he will fall to uphold the grand traditions of Wycliffe Church, and make a name for himself in the town to which he has been called.

ANCIENT ORDER OF FORESTERS

The members of Court Warrington, No. 430, held their annual social at the Blue Lion Hotel, Market Place, on Saturday evening last, when about 70 members sat down to enjoy the excellent catering of Mr. and Mrs. P. E. Lake, the host and hostess.

After tea the tables were cleared and the chair occupied by the D.C.R., Bro. T. Roden, who was supported by C.R. Bro. A. E. Ross, as vice-chair- man, and by P.D.C.R. Councillor Bro. P. Peacock, Councillor John Chrimes, Bro. R. Thornley, D.S., Bro. 0. W. R. Baines, Bro. H. James, Bro. L. Davies, and others. The usual loyal toast was given by the D.C.R., after which a capital musical programme was proceeded with. The Order and the Warrington District was toasted by Councillor Bro. P. Peacock, who referred to the strong position which the District held both financially and numerically. Bro. Thornley, D.S., in replying, thanked the members for the efforts they had put forward to bring about the success of the District in coping with the exigencies of the National Insurance Act.

P.D.C.R. Bro. Baines proposed the toast of Court Warrington, No. 430, and thanked the members for their assistance to him during his term of office as D.C.R. He congratulated the Court upon the election of their two members, Bro. P. Peacock and Bro. John Chrirnes, to the Town Council, and mentioned the interestiag fact that other Foresters on the Council were Aid. Bro. D. Tinnion and Coun. O. Archer. The name of Coun. Bro. John Chrimes, the Court Secretary, was coupled with the toast. Interesting presentations were made during the evening to P.C.R. Bro. A. J. Pettey, for his services as Chief Ranger, and to Bros. J. Jenns, J. Pidgeon, and H. James, Junr., for special services to the Court.

The musical part of the programme was in the capable hands of Bro. A. E. Ross, Mr. Stout, Mr. John Rawes, Mr. Thomson, Mr. Ailelldon, Mr. Dean, and Bro. Roden. Mr. Raves was much applauded for his trombone selections. '

St Paul's Church

One of the most eagerly anticipated social functions in connection with St. Paul's Church is the annual congregational gathering which was held this year on Tuesday evening in the Kendrick-street schools. The programme was made up of an enter- tainment, refreshments. whist drive and dancing. The concert was an excellent one, and the artistes who delighted the large assembly included Miss Bessie Brough, Mrs J Sepliton, Messrs L Aukland (bass), C H Ford (tenor), J Sephton, H M Ashton (elocutionist), W Whittle (humorist.) A quartette party comprising Messrs R Cook„T Lloyd, C Gelderd and L Aukland were loudly applauded for their efforts. A sketch was also given by friends from Emmanuel Church. Mr E Wilkes was an efficient accompanist. The first prize-winners in the whist drive were Mrs Pickton and Mr W Flood.-- The Vicar, in his address, reviewed the work of the church during the past year, and thanked the officers for the way in which they had assisted him.

British Women's Temperance Association. A meeting of the above was held on Wednesday afternoon in 80ld-street School, Mrs Owen Presided', when a very interesting address was ,given by the Rev W Cooper who described the harmful effect of alcohol upon the human system more especially motherhood and the young child. He said that women could be very useful in helping to put down the drink evil becarse the result of it brings so much sorrow and suffering to them. A discussion foil )wed in which the following ladies took part, Mrs Moreland, Mrs Simpson, Mrs Owen, Mrs Moore and Miss Bogstrom. The secretary read the notices and asked all members and friends to give in their names who wished to attend the County Union Conference to be held in Bury on March 12th. National Union of Journalists. On Saturday evening the members of the Warring- ton, Widnes and Runcorn branch of the National Union of Journalists held their annual social gather- ing at the Railway and Commercial Hotel, Widnes. The Widnes contingent had made admirable arrange- ments for the entertainment of the visitors and a highly enjoyable gathering was the result. Mr Frani: Fennnelly (chairman of the branch) presided. After dinner a capital programme of musical and humorous items was gone through, the members contributing being Councillor G Archer, Mr B Baker, Mr Harry Walker and Mr Harry Corrin. Mr T F Wallington was an efficient accompanist. The toast of "The N.LT.J." was honoured on the proposition of the Chairman, who referred to the remarkable progress of the organization and to the strong position which it now occupies. The Secretary of the local branch is Mr E Iddon and the treasurer Mr C E Hawthorn. National Union of Teachers.

The forty-fourth annual Conference of the National Union of Teachers is to be held in Weston-super- Mare during Easter week. Preliminary gatherings sill commence on Thursday, Match 20th. and the Conference will open at 10 a.m. on Monday, March 24th, and close the. following Thursday. The chair will be taken by Mr A W Dakers, B.A. (the President- Elect) of Newcastle-on-Tyne, from whom a Presi- dential Address dealing powerfully with current educational questions may be expected. An importaut programme of motions on educational subjects will be discussed. It is anticipated that 2,000 members will be present, as the appointed representatives of the 521 brandies of the Union. Deputations of teachers from Scotland, Ireland, Belgium, Germany, Sweden Switzerland, the Teachers' Guild of Great Britain and 'lreland, and the Co-operative Union are expected to attend the Conference. The local branch of the be represented by Messrs F H. Longshaw (local secretary), John Upson, John C am, George Newburn, A Malcolm and Miss Upson.

"LIFE ON P TRAWLER."

MR. TOM WING'S, LECTURE AT THE PARR HALL

At the invitation of Mr and Mrs A IT Crosfield, a large audience assembled in the Parr lie Ii last evening to hear a lecture by Mr Tom Wing. ex-M.P. for Grimsby, on "Life on a trawler on Northern Seas." While representing the East-coast sea-port in Parliament, Mr Wing took the opportunity of gaining first hand acquaintance with the fisherman's life by accompany- ing the crew of a trawler on a long and eventful trip. His graphic nariative of his experiences last evening gave his audience an insight into the hard- ships endured by those who "go down to the sea in ships " and it was spiced with a fund of anecdotes which made it all the more interesting. Lantern views of the voyage were also shown. Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD, who had a rousing reception, welcomed Mr. Wing in a short speech, in which he entertainingly recalled a North Sea trip which he him self once made, and also told several delightful stories. "If we are not nautical experts" he continued " there are one or two sea phrases that we all understand. We know what it is to haul the flag to the mast (applause)— and to keep it there—(applause) —and we also realize the difference between steering a straight course towards the port we are bound for, guided by a compass, and smash- ing that compass to pieces. (Laughter and applause) 11l go on much further you will think I sin driving at. something which I,as the tinge of party politics about it. (Laughter.) However, I want to assure Mr Wing that we are all of the right colour here this evening--(applause)— and I would also add how proud lam to have the opportunity of meeting i,o many fine workers for our party." Mr Crosfield went on to say bow highly ho valued the splendid service that was done by the active workers of the party in Warrington and said he was sure they were animtited by the most patriotic motives. This was a day of great pride for all Liberals and of high hopes for the future. (Loud applause.)

QUERIES AND ANSWERS..

OUR READEkS are invited to send ligal queries to be answered week by week. Inquiries must be short, concise, and plainly written, a rid the name and address of the writer given to show that the inquiry is genuine. The replies are by a member of the profession, and every effort is made to secure accuracy, but we cannot assume any responsibility for the opinion or advice given. Mark all envelopes containing queries " Legal," and address them to the EDITOR, EXAMINER,' WARRINGTON. If you do not wish initials to be used in the reply please tuggest a nom-de-1)1111)1c.

5.8.—(1). If 5.13. \yin consider Section 4-1, Sub-sec- tions 1 and 2 more closely he will find that an insured woman who continues to be employed after marriage " shall not be suspended so long as she continues to be so employed " only if she proves that she continues employed. If she chooses not to prove that she continues employed she may become a voluntary contributor under Sub- section 2. Our point was the " proof." (2). What S.B.'s company and some others may do is not the point. Our answer was based on the specilic facts presented to us, the Company concerned being one that did not issue free policies. Our point was that if the agent of a Company which does not do so falsely represents to the assured that a free policy will be given, the premiums paid under a belief in this false statement can be recovered. S.B. may study to advantage The Refuge Assurance Co. v. kettlewell.

P.W.---(1). It is a matter to be settled by the regu- lations of the Approved Society. Generally it will be necessary for the sick visitor to see the sick insured before handing over the sick benefit. (2). The Welshman can claim his benefits given by the Society of which he is a member wherever he lives. Ile may transfer to another section, if he can persuade that other section to accept him. (3). Yes, but the Society is not compelled to accept him. (I). The giving of certificates by doctors for the voluntary benefits is a matter which is generally arranged between the Approved Society and the doctor. Certain Societies have arranged that the doctor shall give their members, if required, one, two, or three certificates free of charge, but if any more are required (the member being also a member of several other Societies) then the doctor is by arrangement entitled to charge for them. But there is nothing in the Act which forbids a doctor to charge for the ceitificate for the voluntary benefits. The better opinion seems to be that a doctor cannot charge for the certificate for the State Insurance. (5). If the Approved Society receives notice of the con- finement within 24 hours, it is thereby made re- sponsible for the- doctor's fee (to an amount not more than 155.) or for the midwife's fee. If notice is not given within 24 hours, the above liability of the Society disappears and they are justified in paying £1 direct to the insured. If after 24 hours but before 12 days have expired a doctor gives notice that he has been called in subse- quently to the confinement, then the Society is responsible for his fee (to an amount not more than 10s.). After 12 days from the confinement the Society is justified in handing over the balance of the 30s. to the insured.

READER.—Yes. The Society can insist on seeing the card and hook of the insured before paying sick pay, because the amount of arrears affects the amount of sick pay. _ . .

H.R.—The widow - must be either Executrix appointed by the husband's wiil or Administratrix where there is no will. Otherwise she cannot sue the debtors if they decline to pay.

HIT.—A distinction must he' made between deduc- tions allowable in calculating what your income is and the relief which may be obtained after the amount of income has been ascertained (e.g.., allowance for insurance premiums, and for chil- dren). The above deductions are set out at length in Form 11 A., which can be obtained from the District Surveyor of Taxes.

INVESTMENT-.—As a matter of fact the money should have been held by the Trustees of the estate and invested by theni for the child's benefit, and the income paid to you as Guardian. In proper cases the Trustees might even advance part of the principal. The Trustees remain liable for the proper investment and use of the money. You have, however, done the best thing in acting as you have done and ,you are entitled to use the income.

I REDUCING LICENCES.

TWO LOCAL HOUSES SUGGESTED FOR COMPENSATION. Application for Off-Licence Refused.

Apart. from an unsuccessful application for an off-licence, there was very little business of public interest at the licensing sessions for the County Borough of W arrington which were held at the Police Court yesterday morning. It was, however, intimated that at the adjourned sessions on Match 7th the Bench would be asked to consider proposals that two licences—those of the King's Arms, Buttermarket- street, and the Railway Tavern, Factory-lane—should be referred for compensation. The magistrates adjudicating were :—The Mayor (in the chair), Alder- men Bennett, Smethuz st and Pierpoint, Councillors Roberts and Wilkinson, Messrs A J Jolley, W Furness, W Roberts, S Wright, J W Hayward, G Piling, C Jackson Holmes, W T Williaws, T Pemberton, T Locker and J Upson.

===== 0002601_19130208_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON'S DRUNKENNESS.

The Chief Constable (Mr Martin Nicholls), in his report to the justices, stated that the number of licence holders in the Borough was as follows :—Alehouses, 78; beerhouses, 94 ; beer and wine "on," 10 ; bEer "off," 51; beer and wine "off," 5; beer, wine and spirits" off; 10; wine and spirits 'off," 3; wine "off." 4; beer, wine, spirits, sweets, &c "off," 4 ; total, 189. These figures showed a reduction of one as compared with tkose of last year. The population of the Borough in 1911 was 72,178, consequently there was one licensed house to every 3818 inhabitants. With reference to drunkenness, there was a decrease of nine in the number of persons proceeded against as compared with the previous year. The figures for the past three years were as follows :-1912, 471 males and 101 females, total 572; 1911, 477 males and 101 females, total 581 ; 1910, 470 males and 114 females, total 584. Of the 572 persons proceeded against 376 were residents of the Borough, and 196 strangers to the town. The days of tte week on which drunkenness occurred were BS follows :—Sunday, 65 ; Monday, 87 ; Tuesday, 54 ; Wednesday, 46 ; Thursday, 49 ; Friday, 58 ' • Saturday, 213. Seventy-one cases occurred in Bridge-street, 38 in Butterrnarket-street, 31 in Winwick- street, 29 in Horsemarket-strest, 16 in Barb-told-street, 15 in Koutsford-road, 14 each at Market Gate and in Sankey-street, 12 in Mersey-street, and 332 in other thoroughfares and public places in the Borough. The actual number of different persons proceeded against was 493, as during the year 36 persons were charged twice each, 10 three times each, 3 four times, 2 five times, and 1 seven times.

A table is appended giving the number of persons pro- ceeded against for drunhennoss in ten northern towns during the year. Birkenhead has the worst record with 1,876 persons proceeded against, representing 14 33 per 1,000 of population. Warrington comes next with the following record :—Proceedeo against, 572 (471 males and 101 females), representing 792 per 1,000 of popula- tion. Burnley has the best figures with 293 persons proceeded against, representing 275 per 1,000 of the population. There are 14 clubs registered in the borough. No complaint had been made to the police against them during the year. No licensee was proceeded against for any offence against the Licensing Laws in the Borough. Nearly 2 ,000 surprise visit of inspection were made by the police to licensed premises during the 12 months, and on a few occasions the attention of the licensees had to be drawn to minor irregularities, but generally the houses had been well conducted.

LICENCES SUGGESTED FOR CONIPENSATION

The whelp of the ale-house licences were renewed, with the exception of that of the King's Arms, Butter- market-street, which was deterred until the adjourned se..?sions. The Chief Constable stated that by arrange- ment with the owners (Messrs. G, eenall, Whitley and Co.) there was a likelihood that the licence would be placed on the conipensation list. When the beer-house licences came up for renewal, the Bench adjourned that of the Railway 'Tavern, Factory-lane, on the same grounds as those put forward by the Chief Constable in the case of the King's Arms.

Mr Browne, on behalf of the owners, consented in both cases.

All the off-licences, music, dancing and billiard licences were renewed.

IMPROVEMENT

AT REFRESHMENT ROOM

The Bench granted an application by Mr Tait, on• behalf of the London and North-Western Bailvray Company, for permission to carry out improvements at the Bank Quay refreshment room. It is proposed to make a door to enable passengers on the new down side platform to enter the room from that side, instead of having to go round the building to the door on the old platform. APPLICATION FOR OFF-LICENCE REFUSED. THE POLICY OF THE BENCH.

Mr H J Longton applied on behalf of Janie Cunning- ham for a beer off-licence to be attached to the brewery premises of Cunningham Bros. in Owen-street, Winwick- road. He pointed out that the brewery had been erected on land adjoining the bottling stores. It was desired to obtain an off-licence for the purpose of taking orders and en undertaking would be given that no jug trade would be done. His instructions were that the sale of drink would not be increased by a single bottle of beer per annum by the granting of the licence. It was simply a matter of business convenience. The premises were rated at £2OO per annum, but under the present conditions they could not do any business in the sale of beer at the brewery. He suggested that the Bench should impose a condition that not less than half a dozen bottles of beer should be sold at n time. Applicant was called and was asked by the Mayor whether, in the event of the application heing granted, he would be prepared to surrender the elf-licence, 29, Froghall-lane. Applicant: That is my brother's licence. Wir Browne (who objected to the application on behalf of five licensees in the neighbourhood) : He is one of the partners in the brewery ? Applicant : Yes. Is it true that there are five licences within a radius of 100 yards of this plice ? -Yes.

Do 'you know :hat there are eight firms already supplying bottled beer in the town ?—Yes. Mr Longton : You don't intend to compete with any licensed house in the d;strict ?—Not at all. It wont increase the sale one bottle.

The Chief Constable said the policy of the bench was that the number of licences in Warrington should, as far as pcssible, be decreased. He had nothing to say with regard to that application but he should strongly oppose any increase of the number of licences, as far as was practicable. A similiar application was granted last year,but the reason why the police offered no objection was that the owner had surrendered three licences in consideration of the removal of one licence to Marsh- house-lane. In this case the-e was no suggestion of a removal or surrender. The police suggested that, unless something of that sort were done,the application should not be granted. The other local brewers were apparently doing all they could to assist the Bench in carrying out reductions. Mr Longton : You have no objection to Mr Cunningham on the ground of character? The Chief Constable : Oh, no. If he were to come to the Bench and say " We will' give up two licences " I should do all 1 possibly could to support the applica- tion.

You oppose the application on the general ground of policy with regard to increasing ths number of licences in the town ?—Undoubtedlv. You are aware that an off-licence is not subject to compensation ?—Oh yes, but once it is obtained you cannot very well do away with it directly. Mr Browne said that if the application were granted it would reduce the number of inhabitants to each licence in the town He opposed it most strenuously, as there was not the slightest necessity for it. The application was refused. TEMPORARY AUTHORITY.

Temporary authority was granted to the following : —Miss Elizabeth McCoy, off-licence, 33, Pierp9int- street ; Mrs Emma Williams, Unicorn len ; William Evans Atherton, off-licence, 19, Gaskell-street.

WYCLIFFE CHURCH.

REV. T. T. JAMES ENTERS UPON HIS PASTORATE.

The first Sunday of February witnessed the com- mencement of a new era in the history of Wycliffe Congregational Church, when the Rev. T. T. James, M.A., formerly of Sheffield, entered upon his pastorate. Seven mouths have now passed since the Rev. John Yong° hrought his long and faithful ministry tt, a close. There were large congregations at both services on Sunday to welcme the new pastor. Devoting a portion of the morning service to the children, Mr. JAMES told a story of Robert Louis Stevenson, illustrating the fact that although he oid not go to Samoa as a missionary but in search of health, yet through his good works and kind Geeds he became a missionary of a very practical kind. The preacher drew the lesson that the children by lives of simple goodness could do much to extend Christ's Kingdom. The sermon was based on the text" The Lord our God be with us as He was with our fathers " (I Kings viii. 57). After a reference to the historic setting of the text and its story of the completion and dedication of the Temple of Solomon, he said no one would be surprised that that congregation should turn to such a passage and let it speak to them as they were beginning their work together as minister and people. He pointed out tat there were a great many men and women who were ready to criticise the church, and sometimes a good deal of wit and wisdom were expended without any great results. There were others who stood outside the church and in a different spirit nod with real serioesness and desire sought to study the particular Purpose the church had to fulfil and the definite function she had to perform. There we-e many within the church's borders who felt inclined so ask how they were to seise certain grave problems with which they were face to face in the history of the church at the present time. They were impressed at times with the sense of the decreasing numbers of those who were attached to any form of recognised religion. Tney had a great statement given in the gospel : "I, If Iba lifted up wiil draw all men unto Me," and if the church of the present day did not attract men they needed to know how and why. It might be because the cross was lacking in our midst. He did not mean a cross in a book but across in men's hearts Sometimes they talked about the alienation of the churches from the great masses of the people and some times it seemed true to them that the great movements of our time and the tendencies of modern life left the church in a backwater untouched and unmoved. He was not saying these things to discourage or dishearten but we ought to face these things. Far from dis- couraging it ought surely to be a matter of encourage- ment to them that they were at least prepared to face the situation and consider the difficulty. The church had done certain things which showed that she had considered the preblem and realised its importance. She had been willing to adapt herself to new methods and new situations. He believed that as never before they were anxious to make the chinch the home of the people. There was en anxiety to welcome within her walls all sorts and conditions of men. All class distinctions that too long disgraced the House of God were fast disappearing. ‘Vorship was becoming lighter, brighter and more gladsome. More than that the Church was bravely seeking to face a new intellectual situation, and was prep ired tore-state the great eternal truth in the light of modern knowledge and modern thought. The Church had begun to speak out. Thank God he believed she had begun to speak at last on the great social evils of the times. There was one thing for which they were waiting and which they could not do without, and that was a new sense of God's own presence. It was the knowledge of God and His real Presence that could inspire them and touch their hearts. He supposed as they were met in that church with its history sacred to many of them, they all felt that their fathers left them a religious heritage which it was theirs to guard over, to honour and to cherish. The preacher passed in review the character- istics of the old puritans, and said it was necessary for us to get back the divine consciousness that made God is great reality to our forefathers. The thing which the church was in the world for and the thing which the church was to give to the world was Geri above every- thing else. The church was not expected to lead the world in science, philosophy, art or politics, but it was expected to present Goo. The preacher proceeded to show that the church was a sanc!uqry and could only fulfil its mission in so far as God wes in its midst. " He setteth me on the high places" (11. Samuel xxii. 34) was the subject of the evening discourse. Ile said this was a song of thanksgiving from the singer, David, who was full of gratitude because religious experience was something real to him. In these days it was necessary to make a re-statement of the value and ideal of Jesus Christ. :Sometimes they were inclined to look with prejudice and criticism upon those who had gone before, but it was questionable whether we in this age were giving anything like the same witness for the iivine love which has redeemed us, or were doing any- thing like the same work or leaking anything like tne same sacrifices as those who had made the history of the east. The preacher proceeded to refer to the sacrifices made for religious freedom, and said it was not so much a criticism of the church that was needed to-day but a new baptism of its heroism and its self-sacrifice. It was as a witness of the faith that he came to that church and be was going to preach a gospel that knew no limit to its power. The love of Christ could redeem and alter, transform end re-establish tha human life. They might tell him that human character was made up of that wonderful wsbwoven by circumstance and environment playing upon hereditary forces, but they must not tell him that it was impossible to introduce into these things another influence that radically changes, transforms, and turns men from evil into good. All history and human experience was against such a contention, for the gospel of Christ knew rio limit to its power. _ _

The choir under the leadership of Mr NV H Payton, Mus. Bee., ably led the singing and also rendered two anthems. Solo parts were sung by Madam Sara Silcock, Miss Hewitt and Messrs NV Booth and P 0 Bailey. After the evening service Mr James presided at the Communion.

THE NEW PASTOR AT THE CONGREGATIONAL GATHERING.

An excellent opportunity of extending a congrega- tional welcome presented itself on Monday evening when the new pastor attended 03 annual tea meeting end concert which took place in the Memorial Buildings The gathering was originally fixed for an earlier date but was postponed in view of Mr James's impending settlement in the town. Over 300 at down to tea, excellent arrangements having been made by Mrs J Hawthorn, Mrs J Ball and Miss K Brown, who were assisted by a willing band of ladies of the church. The tables were presided over by the following : Mrs Z Armitage, Misses Beswick, Brown, Masdames B Farrar, W H Payton, Garland, Longland, Slack Winskill, Misses Porritt, Knowles, Mrs E Wallington and Mrs 3 Walker. The Rev T T JAMES presided at the concert and on rising to make a few opening remarks he had a mos enthusiastic reception. He said it was a happy idea o the friends to have postponed the gathering until tha evening to enable Mrs James and himself to attmd,and it made them feel the welcome to be all the more real and sincere. He had his turn yesterday—(laughter)— hen they spent a pleasant time together. He and his wife were looking forward with hopefulness and courage to the future, and he believed the week-end they had epent in Warrington was the beginning of a very happy time in their work with one another. The members of the congregation bad already made them feel very much at home and they were anxious to make the acquaintance of all among whom their work would be cast. In conclusion Mr James said : Let us put cur trust in God, and I le-lieve there will be great things in store for Wycliffe Church. The organist and choirmaster (Mr W H Payton, Mus. Bac., bad arranged a highly interesting programme which met with great appreciation. Songs were very tastefully rendered by Miss E Hewitt, Messrs W Booth, A Davies, H Hawthorn, P 0 Bailey and J Ainsworth. Miss Hewitt's interpretation of " Daffodils a' blow- ing " was so artistic and effective that an encore was accorded. Mr Bailey was also recalled for his capital song " Lighterman Tom." Miss E Brown gave a reading descriptive of a shipwreck and a gallant lifeboat rescue in graphic style. Mrs Haviour acquitted herself withher usual skill in a john solo. An instrumental trio I for violin, 'cello and piano, by Mrs Haviour, I Miss Boucnier and Mr Payton, was an enjoyable feature. Mr W H Wallington was encored for the stirring way in which be sang "The Laird's Fling.' It is interesting to note—as made known by the Chairman—that Mr Waliicgton sang at the Xelcorne meeting to the Rev John Yonge when he came to Warrington, and at the farewell to him recently, and the audience heartily joined in the Pastor's hope that Mr Wallington might be spared for many more years to entertain the congregation at their social gatherings. The voices of the choir blended well in the part-song "Sleeping " (E German). Mr H M Ashton, as usual created much merriment with his humorous readings The Pastor had to leave for Sheffield before the close of the concert, and the chair was temporarily taken by Mr J W Slack.

Mr GEORGE WALLING TON proposed a comprehensive vote of thanks to the entertainers the ladies who had presided at the tea tables, and all who had assisted to make the function such a pronounced success. This was F ecso nded by Mr DARBYSHIRE and carried, and responded to by Councillor HAWTHORN and Messte J BALL and W BROWN.

OVER ONE MILLION A DAY

Is the number of Beecham's Pills manufactured and sold. Stupendous! But if it were double, it would he better for the health of the people. Of course the demand is growing, for the reason that the pills are what they have always been represented to be—a fine medicinal combination of drugs which corrects many minor ailments and prevents irregularities. Rome was not built in a day, and it has taken sixty years for Beecham's Pills to secure the enormous popu- larity they now enjoy. Real merit is the cause. They recommend themselves, and the sale has not been forced by publication of testimonials.

FIFTY TONS YEARLY are consumed by an appreciative public, in spite of all opposition, misrepre- sentation, and wrong statements concocted to belittle their true worth. Conjecture is in the minds of many as to the effect of the National Insurance Act on patent medicines. For the result we must wait and see, but as regards Beecham's Pills there is little doubt that those who know their value will con- tinue to purchase and recommend to their friends the remedy which they can rely on, which is always at hand. doses of which are of trifling colt and the resort to which will save much time and trouble.

Borough Police Court.

MONDAY—Before the Mayor (in the chair), Aldermen Bolton, Smethurst and Bennett, Messrs A J Jolley, W Furness, S Wright, W Roberts, T Locker and G PiNina. _ _ _

TRANSFERS OF LICENCES.—The following licensing transfers were granted :—Smith's Arms, to John Cheadle ; Bay Horse, to Thomas Roberts ; the Haw- thorn Inn, to John William Plinston ; off-licence, 78, Battersby-lane, to ered Thomas Lashford ; the Howley Hotel, to James Edward Johnson; off-licence, 211, Winwick-road, to John Lockett; the Griffin Hotel, to Tom Fairclough Barber ; the Royal Court Hotel, to George Edward Finch ; the Mersey Hotel, to Michael Burke ; off-licence, 18, Priestley-street, to Charies W Nunnerley. A Fowl, THEFT.—A Di year-old boy named Thomas Gibbin, of 22, Lilford-street, was charged with stealing two fowls value 4s. the property of William Monlsdale, a showman, on the fair ground in Queen-street.—lt was siated that at about 7.20 on Saturday evening the showman saw the defendant under the van in which he, Moulsdale, lived. When asked what he was doing there, the boy said he was tr) ing to find his way out. The man having missed two bantams the Saturday before, taxed the lad who admitted the theft.—The lad was fined lOs and costs, the Mayor remarking that he , had only just escaped being sent to prison. TITURSDAY.—Before Mr It T Fairclough (in the chair) Messrs A J Jolley, W Roberts, J Upson and Dr Naden.

A SPECTACLE CASE.—John Hayes, a pedler, was charged ,% ith attempting to obtain 2s by falso pretnnces from Detective-Sergeant ReddingtOn.—The Chief Constable explained that on Wednesday afternoon Detective it eddington, when in Buttermarket-street, was accosted by the prisoner, who asked him to buy a pair of gold-rimmed spectacles for two shillings. He said he had found them on the road. The officer took prisoner into custody, and from inquiries he ascertained that the spectAcles could be purzhased at 2s f;clper dozen. The prisoner was unaware of the fact that Bedding- ton was a detective, and had evidently taken the officer for a person connected with the Church. Aremand was necessary in order to make inquiries as to the prisoner's tracter.—Prisonor objected to an adjournment, and when the chairman intimated that he would be remanded for seven days, the prisoner replied, "Seven days for nowt."

DRANK EMBROCATION.—Wiiiiam Clarke, an iron worker, residing at 88, Pierpoint-street, Warrington, was charged with attempting to commit suicide by drinking a quantity of embrocation. The wife and children went to bed on Sattyday night, leaving the prisoner asleep on Vl° sofa. S')nrtly afterwards the wife heard her husband shouting," Fetch a policeman ! I have taken poison." Sergeant Woodland was sum- moned and the prisoner was taken to the Infirmary. The bottle was found to contain hartshoru and oil. The wife stated that her husband had suffered from pains in the head, lie had been drinking heavily of late. On promising to sign the pledge and behave himself in the future, the case was adjourned for a month.

DEATH OF A WARRINGTON BANDSMAN.

The death of Mr John Hill, who was prominently identified with the Borough band, took place on Monday, January 276, at his residence, 18, Naylor- street. He was one of the most esteemed tradesmen in the town, and had carried on business in Fennel- street, Buttermarket.street, Bridge-street and Church- street at various times, whilst at one time lie was a Roman Catholic Sunday school teacher and took great interest in parochial affairs. Since 1889 he was a member of the Warrington Borough prize brass band and occupied the post of treasurer to the same. More- over during his musical career he has played with all the bands in the town, was always ready to assist any band which required his services and always directed his utmost energies to the advancement of brass band music. The funeral took place on Saturday last. The cortege left Naylor-street at 3 p.m., beaded by the Warrington Borough and the League of the Cross Prize Brass Bands assisted by representatives from Crosfield's, Penketh Tannery, Peter Walker and Son's, Cheshire Lines, and Earlestown Viaduct Prize Brass Bands, which played the Dead March in " Saul," under Mr T Griffin, ‘N ho made all the musical arrangements. Then behind the bands walked representatives of the League of the Cross and the Ancient Order of Hibernians ; for of each of these societies the deceased was a member. Despite the wretched weather, hundreds of people lined the streets to witness the funeral as it passed on its way to the Cemetery, and even followed to the graveside to pay their last respects to one who was so beloved. The deceased leaves a wife and four children. The principal mourners were :—Mrs Hill, Masters J Bill, T Hill, Miss A Hill and Master Arthur Hill ; also the deceased relatives Mr and Mrs D Houghton and family,Penketh; Mr and Mrs J Taylor, Mr T Corcoran and Mr J Corcoran accompanied by Mr H Rigby, Wilderspool ; Mr J Riley, Sankey-street ; Mr J Hannan, Parker- street and a host of other friends and all the principal bandmasters and bandsmen in the town.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 8, 1913. TOWN COUNCIL MATTERS.

Councillor WILKINSON seconded the amendment. Councillor BROADHURST said Councillor Peacock admitted that the existing engines were obsolete and the remedy which he proposed was worse than the present evil. The committee had decided to employ one motor mechanic and another would be kept in reserve, at a very low cost, at one of the local garages. A motor engine could be playing on a fire in less than two minutes, whereas he had known 25 minutes elapse before the steam engine got to work, because one of the horses had been placed on the wrong side and refused to" draw." (Laughter). Councillor OSBORKE said that they had not been provided with sufficient information to justify the purchase of an engine. It seemed that the motor engine was going to travel so rapidly that it would be on the scene before the water was available. It was a good suggestion that the horse ambulance should he kept at the fire station, so that it could follow the motor engine. (Laughter.) Something should be done with regard to the engines, but to put the present resolution on the egenda was absurd. Councillor PEmBERTON complained that the Council had been very dilatory in dealing with the matter. Councillor PEMI;ERTON We have never made a single offer to Messrs Fairclough. It is intolerable to bear this piffle. The TOWN CLERK said the contractor for the bridge was bound to finish the first part before he began the second. lie (the Town Clerk) was 'assurred that the contractor would not want possession of the site on the south-east side before January 30th. The minutes were passed.

Councilllor ARCHER said the committee had been encouraged in their recommendation by the action of the Chamber of Commerce The fact that tenders had been invited, did not commit them to buy any engine. Warrington ought to be in an independent, position, with regard to its fire engines, and not have to go rolind, cap in hand, asking for the assistance of works brigades. Councillor PEACOCK moved an amendment that the minute of the Fire Engine Committee he referred back. The recommendation to obtain a motor engine was brought forward when the town was in a state of panic, in consequence of Gurton's fire. He agreed that the present engines were not up-to-date,some of the parts of the best engine being inadequate to deal with the larger fires. At the same time, he believed a motar engine was unnecessary in Warrington, where any part I of the town could be reached, under the existing condi- tions, in a few minutes. In addition to paying £1,200 for an engine, they would have to employ two driver- mechanics whose wages would be £4 a week. Then, in order to provide for emergencies, they ought really to have a duplicate engine. He suggested that the committee would better meet the requirements of the town by keeping two or three horses on the premises, providing three steam engines and employing two drivers. It would also be a convenient thing to keep the horse ambulance at the fire station.

ZAM-BUK'S SPLENDID RECORD.

Poisoned Wounds, Burns, Bleeding Piles, Face Sores, Healed in one Family.

Zarn-Buk has achieved a series of wonderful healing triumphs in the home of Mr. and Mrs. J. W. Poole, 55, New Street, Aston Newt,wn, Birmingham. WARRINGTON CVCLING CLUB.

This club held its second hot-pot and smoking con- cert of the winter session on Saturday evening,and the fixture proved to be in every respect one of the most enjoyable and successful social functions ye., under- taken by the club. About 50 members and friends assembled at headquarters (Patten Arms Hotel) at 7.30 and sat down to an excellent hot-pot provided by Mr Burleigh. The musical programme was well up to the standard usually associated with the club gather- ings, and a critical musical audience was highly pleased with the items given and the able rendering of them. The whole of the programme was in the hands of the " Lyric Concert Party," who made their debut, and of their similar successes at other local functions we hope to hear anon. The party comprised an excel- lent combination of voices, and the songs given ware nicely selected to please the taste of a musical ,iudience. The opening quartette "In this hour of ..3:tened splendour" (Pinstcti) by Miss Hastings. V"-dame Lily Fairhurst, and :Messrs J Willett and 7/alker was excep ionally well rendered and showed a 02.1utiful blending of voices. This was followed by a song " Oh, oh, hear the wild winds blow" by Mr Ernest Walker, whose effort was loudly applauded. Mr Walker's other contribution was "The lute player" (Alletson,), for which he received a well-deserved encore, and in response gave a very pleasing Devon- shire song which received so much applause that he was prevailed upon to sing it again. A duet " B4r- earolle from Tales of Haffman (Offenbach) given by Mist Hastings and Maclaine L Fairhurst was well rendered and brought for them much applause. Mr W W Whittle, as the humorist, was in good form and greatly amused his audience with his song "Appetite." His humorous sketch " The Debating Society," given later caused much laughter, and his effort brought for him an encore, to Inch he responded with a song "The Curate," and in which he was seen at his best. Miss Carrie Hastings quite lived up to her reputation as a soprano in the 'Ribbon Song" from "Tom Junes (German), which was delightfully rendered. Her second sonewas " Pleading " (Elgar), for which she received an encore, and responded with a very pleasing song "I hear thee call." It is always a, pleasure to hear Mr J W Willett, and his rendering of "Dear Heart " (3lattei) was highly creditable. Madame Lily Fairhurst was in excellent voice, and gave a fine interpretation of " The Raft," for which she was loudly applauded. Her other item " Within your heart " (Trotere) brought for her an encore, to which she responded with " Annie Laurie." Madame Fairhurst has become one of our most popular local singers, and with ample justification. Mr Percy W Smith acted as accompanist with skill and judgment. The proceedings were brought to a close with a vote of thanks to the artistes.

===== 0002601_19130208_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
UPPER-STOREY WOMEN.

In Greek literature there appear frequent allusions to the habits of women. They seemed to have lived mostly in the upper storeys of the houses. Pictures on vases also suggest this separation of women and men, as the former are often shown sitting up above. In those days the man was recognised as the superior being. Now, all that is changed, and woman occupies her proper place in society. Some Grecian customs still remain ; and fortunately they are good ones. The use of the Currant may be cited as an example. From time immemorial, Currants, the dried grapes ;rom Greece, had been used as the food of man. They have always had a deservedly high reputation,because of their delicious flavour and wonderful nutritive properties. There are innumerable ways of using Currants.. If you appreciate a special treat combined with economy, try this recipe. SUET PUDDING (PLAIN). currants, 121 b beef suet finely chopped, lib flour, pint milk, lemon, fresh butter cr cream, sugar. METHOD. Mix all the dry ingredients in a basin, add the milk, and work into a fairly stiff dough. Put the mixture into a floured cloth, tie up and boil gently for 21, hours. Serve with quarters of lemon, fresh butter or cream, and sugar (moist or castor).

A burglar entered the premises belonging to Will Emu Gordon, tobacconist and sub-postmaater,Victoria-road, Widnes, during the early hours of Sunday morning- The place was secure at midnight, but an entrance wag effected from the back by releasing the catch of the kitchen window. The occupants were not disturbed. though at the bottom of into stairs the panel wa9 removed from the door leading the shop. FullY forty holes were bored round the panel, which was large enough to admit a man body. The safe, contain- ing £2OO of postal orders, stamps, and money was not tampered with. 'I he thief or thieves ransacked tha shop of the most valuable smokers' requisites,and anew ‘cash register, valued at £25, was damaged badly and the £l3 contents abstracted. Altogether the loss is estimated at about £6O. The robbery was evidently carried out by a novice. The boring in the door must have taken an hour to accomplish with a joiner's hit. Bent chisels and a screw-driver, the property of Mr Gordon, were found lying about.

RUNCORN URBAN COUNCIL. "NEW TERROR TO PUBLIC LIFE."

At the Runcorn Urban Council on Tuesday evening twenty-one deaths and forty births were recorded for the month, as compared with thirty-eight births and nineteen deaths for January of last year. Mr Baker complained of the proposed expenditure in fencing Moughland-lane, which has been widened. There was no necessity to purchase 370 yards of fencing. The railings the council had in stock might be utilised. Mr Imison said that when the council had decided upon a certain line of policy, it was usual that resolu- tions which simply carried the policy into effect should be allowed to pass without opposition. Mr Baker had added a new terror to public life. At the council meetings he took voluminous notes, and, months later, informed his fellow-members that, at a certain time on a certain day, they said so and so. Laughter.) Mr Baker took everything seriously, and members must be serious too, recollecting that there was a chief among them takin' notes."

i—..-• ••• MI mor ANTIQUARIAN CHAPTERS

"WHO KILLED COCK ROBIN ?"

In taking note of the French, Irish, Welsh, Manx, and English rhymes concerning the wren we saw that they all point to the ceremonial of ancient bird sacrifice, a practice that belongs to primitive man and that has survived from a period long before the dawn of history.

But tile wren is not the only bird that appears to have been sacrificed. There are songs that describe the blackbird as being offered and eaten at the dawn of the year, a habit which harmonises curiously with the eating of blackbird pie in many English counties on Twelfth Night (January 6th). The robin, too was killed and sacrificially eaten in France, on February Ist, doubtless in the same way as the wren. Though we have no record of robin sacrifices in our own land, there is an old Scotch rhyme which almost certainly refers to the custom.lt is called "The Robin's Testament" and in a quaint form reveals the robin making his will and bequeathing the various p-trts of his body. Here is the poem : Guid day now Bonnie Robin How lang have you been hero? I've been bird about this bush This mair than twenty year. CHORUS : Teetle ell ell, teetle Eldie. But now I am the sickest bird That ever sat on brier And I wad make my testament Cruidman, if ye wad hear. Gar tak this bonnie nib o' mine, That picks upon the corn, And gie' t to the Duke of Etamilton To be a hunting horn. Gar tak these bonnie feathers o' mine, The feathers o' my nob And gie to the Lady o' Hamilton, To fill a feather bed. Gar tak these bonnie feathers o' mine Tha ftathers o' my breast, And gie to ony bonny lad That'll bring me to a priest. Gar tak this guid right leg o' mine And mend the brig o' lay; It will be a post and pillar guid, It will nei her ban nor gae. And tak this other leg o' mine And mend the brig o' Weir, It will be a post and pillar guid It'll neither ban nor steer. Robin made his testament Upon a coil of hay, And by came a gretdy gled And snapt him a' away.

The lines have obviously undergone changes with repetition. But the foundation of the poem can only be the one we.have suggested.

===== 0002601_19130208_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
A CURIOUS CHURCH SURVIVAL.

As in the case of many other ancient practices the church took over the usage and turned it to account. Let us compare "The Testament of the Robin " with "The Testa- ment of the Ass" a Catholic ceremonial which survived until past the middle of the 17th century. _

In many French cities it was called "The Feast of the Ass" and consisted in bringing a donkey into the church whilst there was read from the Service Book passages which set forth that an ass had carried the Holy Family to Egypt and had borne Christ into Jerusalam. The response consisted of the braying of the assembled multitude. The ass was then led outside and a dialogue piece was read. The beast was asked to whom he left the various parts of his body. He was supposed to reply that he left his skin to the drummer, his tail to the priest to make an aspergill, his legs to make pillars in the cathedral, and his feet, head and teeth for various other purposes. This is curiously like "the Testament of the Robin" and both echo the very ancient belief that every part of a sacrificed creature had magical powers.

===== 0002601_19130208_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
BIRD WEDDINGS.

The next thing to notice in our attempt to understand "Cock Robin" is that in many old rhymes the robin and the wren are coupled together.

We have, for instance, the Lancashire distich : The robin and the wren are God's cock and hen, E pink and the sparrow are the devil's bow and arrow.

If there were space we could quote rhymes from every corner which join the robin and wren, and not a few give the sparrow a bow and arrow. Most proverbial lines join the robin and wren in friendship and several poems describe their marriage. These last are the most interesting, and as they belong to both France and Spain as well as England we had better take note of them. In the French story the new married couple are short of food, so the gad-fly brings a loaf, the gnat brings a cask, the butterfly a joint and the sparrow some grapes, the flea jumps out of bed and dances with the louse. In the Spanish version the same thing happens, but in addition the rat comes out to play the fiddle. When the fun is at its height the cat spoils everything ,y seizing and killing the rat. The English verses are different and describe the actual ceremony. The lark sings, the cock blows the horn, and Parson Rook carries Mother Hubbard's book, the linnett and blackbird also officiate, and the bullfinch tolls the bell.

At the dinner which follows the raven brings walnuts, the dog Tray brings a bone, the pigeon grain, and the owl brings a sack of flour. The fun flows fast and furious when unhappily the cuckoo appears: When in came the cuckoo and made a great rout, He caught hold of Jenny and pulled her about. Cock robin was angry and so was the sparrow, Who now is preparing his bow and his arrow. His aim then he took, but he took it not right, His skill was not good, or he shot in a fright ; For the cuckoo he missed, but cock robin he killed, And all the birds mourned that his blood was so spilled.

This is decidedly interesting. It shows us two of the sacred birds of our ancestors getting married. The unsocial cuckoo, which as all students know, was anciently the enemy of married bliss, comes in and breaks the happiness. The sparrow who kills Cock Robin does so by accident, while aiming an arrow at the cuckoo.

It shows us, too, the birds each bringing something to the wedding feast after the pattern of the communal feasts of our remote ancestors.

TO REPAY A LOAN.

HUSBAND AND THE MATERNITY BENEFIT. " What tecame of the maternity benefit" was the question raised at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday during the hearing of a prosecution taken at the instance of the N.S.P.O C. The Alayor presided and the defendant Peter Hanlon, 8, Heaton-square, Orford-street, was charged with having assaulted his stepdaughter, Mary Ann Taylor, 15 years tof age. Mr Browne, who prosecuted, stated that the defendant was a carter in the employ of a local contractor and earned 20s per week. His step-child worked at Cockhedge factory and earned from 5s to Os per week On Wednesday last the wife was confined and the bed was brought downstairs. They had received 309 maternity benefit under the Insurance Act but on Saturday afternoon the defendant did not come home until 4.30 and he was then under the influence of drink. He complained that ,the fire was low and that his dinner was not ready. The wife never knew what time he was coming home on Saturday afternoons but there was meat on the table. The wife tried to calm him but he got hold of the girl's hair, pulled her about, and struck her about the head. The sick mother did everything she could to stop him and eventually succeeded. The defendant went out but returned soon afterwards, and ill-treated the girl again, thumping her on the head and hitting her on the body. The mother screamed and neighbours entered the house and rescued the girl. The defendant came home on Saturday afternoons in a very nasty humour. The wife had had to stay out on Saturday nights. When arrested defendant said "It is correct."

The Mayor referred to the maternity benefit and said he would like to know what had become of the money. Mr Browne: The defendant drew the money and handed 2Ss to his wife.

The Mayor : What became of the 2s ? Prisoner : I used it to pay back a lona. The Mayor : That has nothing to do with it. The money was not intended for your use. It was meant entirely for your wife. Inspector Wainwright gave evidence to the effect that he had visited the house and found the girl in a distressing condition, and there was an abrasion over the right temple and scratches on the arm, The Mayor : Are you satisfied that the 283 was properly administered ? Inspector Wainwright: Yes. The defendant had grumbled on Saturday at giving his wife us 6d out of his wages. He remarked that she had had 28s and she ought to be satisfied. He said he had paid for it. The Mayor : Such matters are hardly within the purview of this court, but I think we ought to know, it will have a good effect in other cases.

The Defendant said he would be better away from his wife and family. He would be willing to contribute to their maintenance under a separation order. He struck the girl with his open hand because she called him Dames.

REN'S CORNER,

:d by UNCLE CALEB.

'ABLE.

A ragged beggar was creeping along the street. He carried an old wallet and asked every passer-by for a few cents. As he was grumbling at his lot, he kept wondering why it was that people who had so much were never satisfied, but were always wanting more.

" If I only had enough to eat and wear, I should be satisfied," said the beggar. Just at that moment Fortune came down the street. She saw the beggar and stopped. She said to him:

" Listen : I have long wished to help you. Hold out your wallet, and I will pour this gold into it, but I will pour only on this con- dition: all that falls upon the ground shall become dust. Do you understand ?" "Oh, yes, I understand," said the beggar. "Then have a care," said Fortune: "your wallet is old."

-He opened the wallet quickly, and the yellow metal was soon pouring in. "Is that enough ?" "Not yet," said the 'beggar. "Isn't it cracking ?" asked Fortune. "Never fear. Just a little more," said the beggar ; "just another handful.—Another —Another." lhe handfuls were added And the wallet burst from end to end. AUTOMOBILIA. Ten men walking along the street, Hailing the joys that mortals meet; Comes a motor of swift design— Now, alas! there are only nine. Nine men crossing the public way, Full of the joy of the golden day; Sounds the horn a bit too late— Now, dear me! there are only eight. Eight young children upon the road, Playing in front of their abode: Comes a smart recruiter of heaven— Now, you note, there are only seven. Seven men crossing the busy street, Little knowing what they will meet: C(,mes a craft of the River Styx - Now, if yUu count, there are only six.

Six men running a touring car, Ponder not how safe, but far; One of them reaches home alive Hospitals shelter the other five. Five joy-riders unsafe to meet, Riding amuck in the midnight street, Fifty miles per the hour or more: Now there are merely parts of four.

One poor fellow who stays at home, Never abroad in the streets to roam : He is in the invalid ranks— Just in the land of the living, thanks! WILL CARLETON. THIS WEEK'S STORY. SOMETHING MISSING IN THE PICTURE. '

And then 1 showed the children a large picture that illustrated the text in bright colours.

DECLARATION.

" National Scrvic:-., is coming " was the striking declaration of Captain Crosflerld at the annual supper an i presentation of shooting prizes held in the large dining room of Messrs Cros field's works an Thursday last week. About 140 adherents of the League sat down to an excellent dinner, catered for by the cookery staff of the works. An admirable musical progremme wes arranged by the committee, guided by the energetic secretary, Mr Ernest Ball. Solos were rendered by Messrs E Pownall, J Houghton, H Bennett, and were appreciatively received, as was also a concertina solo by Mr NV Brobbin. Mr H M Ashton delighted the gather- ing with his humorous recitals. Mr Bert Smith was an etticent accompanist. . . . _

The following is a list of the pr:ze winners :—Open competition : Messrs J Grosvenor, J Sankey, H Potts, sen., G W Limb, E Pollard, E Bal,, A R 3 de, T Taylor. Old soldiers competion : Messrs J Plinston, A Adams, H Whitfield, Sergeant-Instructor J Allardice Civilian Competition: Messrs H Potts, jun.,A Bowers' J Broady, C Ryder. Christmas Competition : Alessrs, H Potts, jun , Sergt-Ins. J Allardice, C P Day, .1 Grosvenor, Hutcheson, A Shepherd, J Towers, E Ball, S Jamcs. J Yarwood, J Ravenscroft, W G Limb, J W Elliott, AT Plinston.

Captain CROSFIELD, in submitting the toast of " The President, Lord Roberts," said he thought they must all feel that it was a great privilege to have such an illustrious man at their head. Now,after spending some- thing like fifty-one scars in the service of his country when he might well rest, he was prepared to go forth to raise the standard once again waging the war against the lethargy and apathy of so many people in the coun- try. Lord Roberts knew better than anyone of the danger of unpreparedness and the horrors and miseries of war for a civilian population. At his great age this gallant soldierasho bad nothing to get from the British people, went forth up and down the country, giving up his leisure, time, and health, to bring home to the people of this country, that it was not right that they should go on living in that state of unpreparedness. (Applause.) It WttB the desire of Lord Roberts that the military training which Lord Haldane said was necessary should be given before war broke out,and not after. How long would this purbliasl nation go on with this system of training after a war had gone on ! It was a ridiculous and amazing fact that a nation which prided itself on its wisdom should not listen to such proposals. They required no great brains on the subject it was common-sense to do the training before mid not afterwaids. (Hear, hear.) To accomplish that they must accept National Service because they could not work it on a voluntary basis. They could not get employers to let their men off from work unless all the employers were in the same boat. Rich and poor must do an equal share and the move- ment was an instance where wealth had no privilege. Another important condition in the scheme was that the citizens army was not to be used to pat down strikes. Under the scheme everyone had to go through the ranks and promotion was only by merit. Equal opportunity for all " was the motto in this citizen army. (Applause.) Unless they got the nation to agree to a compulsory system, they could not work on these broad democratic lines, and they would have to continue with the Territorial Army, without those added advantages. They felt with Lord Roberts in saying that they must put the Territorial Army on a sound basis and the sooner it was done the better it would be for everyone concerned. Ile hoped they w uld do their duty to their noble president by rallying round the standard which he had raised and would work for him end the cause during the year with renewed power. (Applause.) Mrs B S TOWNROB distributed the prizes to the successful Service Leaguers in the shooting competi- tions. In a few felicitious phrases she remarkeo that the past year bad been one of great achievement for the National Service League. A year ago they were engaged in a great war against apathy and to-day they could say witri conVence that they had made great gaps in the wall of their opponents. The whole fabric was now tottering under their attack teed they could look to the coming year to secure the achieve ment of their aims and the welfare of our manhood. (Applause). Mr G DALE, in moving a comprehe naive vote of thanks to Mrs Townroe, the Shooting Committee end the artistes' said that vvhilst they wanted the service of the men for the defence of their homes and country they also desired the sympathy and services of the ladies. It was by the wsistance and cosmicl of the ladies that that great cause could he helped femora. The health of the President of the Werth gton Branch (Mr T II Lyon) WEIS submitted by Mr B S TowNnoF, who paler a tribute to the generosity and genuine interest which Mr Lyon took in the affairs of the League. Warrington he said had played its part in bringing that great reform to the fore-front and thei r splendid work bad been of invaluable service to the League throughout the country. Two years ago they urged forward the necessity of invoking the aid of the trade unionists of the country as it would be making a step for advancen:ent. He had add reused many meetings in various parts of the country and he had been astornaled by the way many of the leading trade unionists had expressed themselves in favour of the system of National Service. Although there might be disagreement en the question of universal training in the Territorial Army, on one point he had never heard one word of disagreement. Every trade unionist was in favour of training in cadet corps—the training of every youth in the countra . In his opinion every youth should be trained from the age of fourteen to, eighteen. It was a matter of national importance to-day that a youth should choose a career for which he was best suited by his ability and character- Wicked politicians talked a great deal about economy of national money but what they required most was economy of our national brains and the National Service League would achieve that. Ile believed when National Service came into operation they would be a more united people, and look to the future when the youths of the country would march forward, not with hands in their pockets and cigarettes in their mouths, but with heads erect to face the battle of life. (Applause). Mr E BALL, on behalf of the Committee, responded to the vote of thanks.

On the proposition of Lieutenant SF,YMOUR JONES, a vote of tkanks was accorded the chairman (Captain Crostield. _ _ .

Captain CROSFIF,LD briefly responded, and said it might seem a rrad ide4 for a body of fellows like them- selves to fire England for National Service, but it was coming. The work they had done would be largely responsible for the success of the movement. (Applause)

SOUTHERN ONTARIO.

Bordering on the great lakes with a climate benefited by the ametiorating infllences of these large bodies of water containing suitable soils for intensive farming, is a portion of the province of Ontario, 1411iCh is secur- ing grf at attention at the present time. This excellent combination of conditions offers opportunities of various kinds. The man with a small farm ranging from 10 to 25 acres can, by applying scientific systematic methods, produce crops which will yield a return of from £lO to £2OO par acre. These returns are greatly enhanced in those districts where the farmers are using co-operative methods of marketing. They supply a uniform product, eliminate the expenses of the middle- man and securing a reputation for their district. The advantage to the investor in such a district is that he can buy his land at about the price of £2O per acre, making a very good living while developing their land, and are able to realizs from three to four times the original cost in as many years. Thera are and will be enormous opportunities for some time in these parti- cular districts for men from Great Britain with capital ranging from £1,050 to £5,000.

AN INSURANCE POINT.

In the llouse of Commons on Monday, Viscount Wolmer (Newton C.) asked the Chancellor of the Exchequer whether a man who had been injured in the course of his employment, and who had had to lay up for one week, could obtain sickness benefit after the first three days under the National Insurance Act, since he was net entitled to cohlpensation under the Workmen's Compensation Act because he had not been ill for a_fortnight. _

Mr Masternian : Yes, sickness benefit is payable when the necessary conditions are satisfied for any p3riod of incapacity in respect of which the insured person is not entitled to compensation under the Workmen's Compensation Act.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

THE MOBUS PROJECT. It is probably as well that three or four members of the Town Council should inspect and try the motor buses before deciding upon the type most suitable for the locality. Still, I happen to know that it is possible for even aldermen and councillors to be deceived. I once accompanied such a deputation on a precisely similar errand. One of the firms in which they were in negotiation was particularly persistent and con- fident. They invited us to test their bus most severely, and we ran it up hill and down dale for fully thirty miles. It behaved sßlendidly, and it was unhesitat- ingly decided to recommend the purchase. Neverthe- less, somehow the bus that had made so good an impression eventually proved the most costly and least satisfactory iu the whole fleet. A FARCE.

I have conceded that Aldermen Smethurst and John Evans and Councillors Wilkinson and Peacock may do good service on the occasion now especially referred to. However, I hive thought sometimes that public bodies never want for an excuse for a jaunt. I have known a Town Council practically agree upon the appointment of an official—say, gas engineer or rnar.ager of electricity works, and then sanction a visit on the part of the committee chiefly concerned to the place whence he came! ruE ÜBIQUITOUS SCOT.

Instance; would doubtless present themselves to the minds of most subscribers to this paper as they read What Councillor Locker had to say on this subject on 'Burns Night." I knew one Scotsman who came into Lancashire to manage a motor 'bus department for a eettain Corporation. After a while he had a tempting Oiler to do similar work in Constantinople, and Promptly made a twelve months' agreement. At the end of the term he went to South Africa, and is now doing exceedingly well in Johannesberg. Then see what a lot of Scots doctors we have in this country. Poor parents there pinch and save in order that their sons may have the best preparation for life's wcrk,and I believe Edinburgh University is the cheapest in existence. THE OLD GIBE.

I recently attended a Secondary School speech day is the neighbourhood of Manchester, and the principal speaker, an eminent University don, said: When the average boy leaves school at the age of fourteen or so, he throws his books away—unless he is a Scot, and in that case he sells them. " CLANNISH. The clannishness of the Scotch enabled the Chief Constable to give a bit of experience at the expense of the Caledonian Association. I once met a some- what similar specimen at the Royal Eye Hospital, Manchester. He was of the tramp class, but owing to the fact that two or three influential members of the staff of the institution were Scots, he lived like the proverbial fighting cock all the time he was there. METAPHYSICS. _ .

_ The fondness of the Scotsman for metaphysics reminds me of a miner's definition. He said : "Two teen are discussing ; neither of them knows what he is talking about, neither do the hearers understand a Word of it all—that's metaphysics." PRETTY ROUGH.

4 . university extension lecturer of my acquaintance tells me a good story against himself. It was his first experience of a mining district, and be thought he was doing very well indeed, when a collier walked to the front of the platform and said "Young man, I don't blame you, you've done your best, but those who sent You here ought to be hanged ! In bis lecture on trade in the middle ages, Mr Dalby Rave instances of systematic dishonesty, particularly in regard to hay and wool.
