" SUNRISE. "

ALDERMAN BENNETT'S ANNUAL OUTBURST.

[By C. E. Parker.]

The "Sunrise Annual" which has just appeared is a very readable publication, and contains admirable comments on the principal events, national and local, which occurred in 1912. Perhaps the greatest interest of this publication is that it gives us an annual insight into the versatile mind of Alderman Bennett, and we think we are justified in calling it "Alderman Bennett's Annual." The introductory poem on Canada is worthy of Rudyard Kipling. It is as full of noble sentiments as it is of questionable state- ments masquerading as facts. We are proud of our local poet even when he appears to us to convey economic absurdities in Imperial- istic verse. The poem concludes: "Fools are we all and traitors everyone, If we the chance should miss." In prose language the chance we are not to miss is that of putting a tax upon all we need from abroad for the benefit of the wheat industry in Canada, those engaged in the same having plainly said they do not want such taxes imposed. But it is too bad to try and lower the flight and interrupt the song of our local lark ; his home is in the heavens and to attempt to bring him down to -earth is a sorry task.

_ The main article "The progress of the year "is written by the same pen. It reveals, what is rare in these days when party divi- sions are so strongly marked, an independ- ence of mind which (whether we agree or differ) is very refreshing. The Alderman is nothing if not original. He condemns the Welsh Disestablishment Bill because it does not hand over a substantial slice of the endowments given prior to 1662 to the Non- conformist bodies which came into existence after 1662. While favouring such a plan he adds "We at any rate as Nonconformists will not countenance the plan." That is the plan in the Bill. Alderman Bennett has lost touch with the spirit of Nonconformists if he thinks they desire, or would accept, any of the endowments in question. To the Non- conformists this is a national question and they approach it from the standpoint of citizens. Before the Alderman speaks as " We " he should be sure he represents any- one but himself in making a claim on behalf of Nonconformists for national church endowments. In regard to Home Rule the Alderman thinks the Irish people should be denied the form of Home Rule they desire, and be made to !accept a form the Alderman has thought out. He calls for some sort of reference to the country on Home Rule, but being a food taxer, naturally does not see much need of a Referendum on Food Taxes.

That the Alierman should, in poetry and prose give expression to sentiments antagonistic to some of the Acts of the Liberal Party was to be expected. But if Mr. Bennett lashes the Liberal Party and its Government with whips, when he comes to his own party he uses scorpions. For example, speaking of the attitude of the Conservative party on the Insurance Act, he says" We have seen the astounding spectacle of a great party who acclaimed it with exaggerated praises at the outset, finding that they might make party capital out of its defects, assailing it with a bitter and an unscrupulous animosity not often paralleled in all the squalid annals of party warfare." But the Alderman reserves his strongest censure for the recent action of the Conserva- tive party in regard to the practical abanddn- ment of the proposed Food Taxes. He says, "And what shall we say of the latest mani- festation of political cowardice and inepti- tude? " After this breezy start conies a description of the Tory Leader's Albert Hall statements P. bout Food Taxes. He then pro- ceeds : `.` It seemed a sensible and workable suggestion, but . suddenly a great provincial daily began to vigorously assert that we could not possibly win unless we either dropped the Food Taxes or submitted Tariff Reform to a Referendum. . What malign influence prompted this unfortunate and ill-timed declaration we cannot tell. But all the poltroons of the party, on the platform and in the press, have been tumbling over each other ever since in their anxiety to disclaim the policy which has for ten years been 'the main constructive plank in the Tory plat- form." To calmly propose, as some of our would- be leaders do, to drop Food Taxes altogether is a policy only worthy of a party which is destitute of either principle or honour. i Any criticism we would be nclined. to make on the recent evidence of opportunism of the Tariff Reform party would looks pale when placed beside the lurid words of the local Tariff Reform President. Alderman Bennett has become a perplexing problem to his own party. They thought by placing him on the box seat of the Tariff Reform coach be would be made to realize his responsibility and obey party orders,so when they tell him to turn aside from the straight and only road to Colonial Preference, upon which the coach has been travelling for the last ten years he refuses, and says the orders are due to "malign influence, and the policy destitute of principle or honour," The Alderman's position locally is very similar to that of his national leader, they both have got their orders from the party to turn aside from the straight road into that which leads to the Protectionist abyss. Mr. Bonar Law (with a bad grace it is true)pulls hard on the reins and turns down the new road. It will be interesting to see whether our Warring- ton Tariff Reform leader will obey the pro- prietors of the local coach, or whether he will attempt to drive straight on in spito of them and perchance perish in the attempt. As to this we must" wait and see." For the present the coach is at a standstill, the trailer—the Tariff Reform van—is sent to rest in its shed, the voice of its orators are no longer heard in the streets, and there is a blessed calm at Bridge Foot.

MOTOR FIRE ENGINE FOR LANCASTER. A powerful motor fire engine has just been put into service at Lancaster. It is of the Merryweather petrol " Hatfield" type, with pumps to deliver 450 gallons per minute, and can travel at 40 miles an hour on the level. In addition to ample accommodation for fire- men, hose and gear, the ruachino also carries a 50ft., fire escape, and a special feature of the equipment is a 50 gallon tank from which the plump can draw a supply of water and throw a lin. jet for " first-aid " work. At the official tests last week the engine amply proved its capabilities. Starting with the "first-aid " jet, one, two, three and four jets were afterwards shown at work. In one of the tests with the pump drawing direct from the water main and employing a lin. jet, a pressure of 2051b5. per square inch was secured, thus amply proving the capacity of the pump to augment the existing town water pressure.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 8, 1913.

The story of the funeral harmonises with the rest. It is a " communal " funeral in which all show homage to the dead bird. There is no printed version of our familiar "Who killed Cock Robin ? " until 1744. As to its age we cannot guess, but it stands here in printed form quite a finished pro- duct. Nor has it changed very much since. Here are the four first verses. Who did kill Cock Robin? I said the sparrow, with my bow and arrow And I did kill Cock Robin. Who did see him (lie? I said the fly, with my little eye, And I did see him die.

And who did catch his blood ? I said the fish, with my little dish, And I caught his blood, And who did make his shroud ? I said the beetle, with my thread and needle, And I did make his shroud.

The bullfinch (not the bull) tolls the bell and the concluding refrain runs: All the birds of the air Fell to sighing and sobbing, When they heard the bell toll For poor Cock Robin. Little pathetic ditties which make the birds take part at funerals are common to Italy, Germany, Spain and France. The German says: Who is dead? The pauncr. Who digs his grave? The cuckoo. Who rings the bell? The stork. Who'll be the scholar? The Pee-wit, &c.

The Spanish makes the cat carry the coffin and the ravens ring the bell, while the partridge bears the mourning.

A SUMMARY AND A GUESS.

We do not suggest that the Cock Robin dirge as we now have it is of great antiquity, but our recital of the points of interest in connection with bird sacrifice has probably convinced our readers that it contains a very definite echo.

Let us recapitulate. One of the earliest religious habits of our ancestors was the sacrifice of sacred birds, of which the wren was one.

The wren has been hunted and sacrificed with a traditional ceremonial until our own day. _ _

Throughout Western Europe this has been observed at the winter solstice of the turn of the year. .

Carvings in Egypt show that bird sacrifice was practiced there 18 centuries ,before the Christian era and that the idea of the birds sorrowing over a victim was present then. Nursery rhymes exist which show that the victims were cut into pieces, each part down to the last feather being regarded as precious, witnesses attesting to the death of the victim.

The robin along with the wren was sacri- ficed as shown in " The testament of the Robin."

Throughout Western Europe there are rhymes and songs which are in the nature of dirges over dead or sacrificed birds. Possibly we shall be right if we regard "Who killed Cock Robin" as a playful survival of what was once a serious ritual.

The dirge sung o—N-T-er the sacred birds at their sacrifice may be reflected in it, or it may be part of a longer recital which told that Interesting bit of bird-lore the marriage of " God's cock and hen," the sacred wren and robin. Perhaps an early part of the poem describing the wedding and the intru- sion of the cuckoo is lost and we have retained only the funeral dirge which con- cludes the story. But we have strayed into the realm of con- jecture and must cease. If our readers can take the facts and form a better theory it will be pleasant to hear from them. A. M.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 8, 1913.

DEFYING THE MAYOR.

ANGRY SCENES AT THE COUNCIL MEETING.

Resentment.

Threat to Close the Meeting.

Fever heat is rapidly becoming the normal tempera- ture of the Warrington Town Council. The monthly meeting on Tuesday evening was characterised by even angrier scenes than took place last month and at one stage it locked as if a deadlock would be reached, the Mayor threatening to close the meeting, if certain members did not obey his ruling. The trouble arose over the purchase of properties required for street improvements. The prices to which exception was taken appeared on the private agenda, and the Mayor ruled that, in the interests cf the Corporation, they could not be discu.sed publicly. Councillor Pemberton promptly challenged the ruling and a brisk passage-at-arms resulted. Councillor Gough joined in the fray and was sternly ordered by the Mayor to "sit down." Ultimately Councillor Pemberton bowed to the Mayor's decision, but said he should take steps to acquaint the public with the facts concerning the purchases, which he regarded as improper. In a sub- sequent discu.sion on the suggested provision of a new organ for the Parr Hall, Councillor Gough again came into collision with the chair, and was threatened with ejection. So protracted were the debates, that at 9.30 part of the business—chiefly a proposition by Coun- cillor Osborne that an official shorthand writer to the Corporation be appointed—was deferred until the next meeting.

MR. F. NEILSON, M.P., AND THE FREE CHURCH MOVEMENT.

Mr F Neilson, M.P., who opened a Wesleyan bazsar at Almondbury, Huddersfield, on Saturday, said that the Free Church movement was indeed a mighty one, end its people, afflicted by the low wage system, had done marvellous things. If they brought God's truth on earth they would abolish poverty and the diseases attendant upon it. They could establish Heaven upon earth, but it would be as far off in the next two thousand years as in the past two thousand years unless the Churches made up their mind to go for the truth. God never meant such a world to endure as the one we had to-day.

WOMEN'S GHAT.

Hotels for Working Women.—What Lord Rowton did in establishing chei,p lodging houses for men, others are doing for women. Sir Algernon West suggested to the late Mrs Lewis, widow of the late Mr Samuel Lewis, that she might usefully devote some of her vast, wealth to his enterprise, but although she did nothing in this direction during,her lifetime, she left large sums of money for the erection and maintenance of homes for women to be called "The Ada Lewis Lodging Houses for Women." The first of these institutions,in the New Kent-road, in London, was formerly opened the other day by Princess. Louise, Duchess of Argyle, and will shortly be ready to accommodate close upon 250 women. The work girl as a rule, dislikes the word " home," and this latest attempt to solve the problem of cheap board and lodging for working women might might well be called an hotel. The cubicles each with a separate window, are all bright and airy, 'fitted with drawers and cupboard, warmed by radiators, and lit by electric light. There are spacious bathrooms, kitchen and laundry-room arrangements,seParate apartments for sewing and clothes-brushing, and in the dining room, cheap and good meals may be purchased at a restaurant bar or residents can cook their own food free of expense in the kitchen. Every possible provision is made for cleanliness, healthfulness and comfort, at a cost to the residents of 6d. per night or 35. per week. Women Rifle Shots.—Recent years have seen the establishment of several Ladies' Rifle Clubs, and year by year a smsll number of women are among the com- petitors at 13isley, where they mostly make high scores. At range firing, strength and physique are not important factors, and many women make as good shots as men. To put this to the test, a team of women, captained by Miss Eva Fenton, challenged a team of men to a shoot- ing competition at the Imperial Rifle Club in London, the conditions being ten shots at twenty-five yards. All were picked shots, and the women only lost the match by five points, tbe scores being : men 781, women 776.

Solace from the Weed.—The tobacco habit raises all sorts of controversies. Like the women's suffrage question, it divides all parties and all classes. Should the bishops smoke ? Should the clergy indulge in such a habit? Should women smoke? and should they be allowed in the smoking compartments on the railways? These questions are all provoking lively discussions en both sides of the Atlantic. A Philadelphia rector has got into hot water for saying that all people who per- mit women to smoke at their entertainments 'ought to be tarred and feathered. On this side we have Dr Boyd Carpenter, Canon of Westminster, and former Bishop of Ripon, saying that he sees no objection to the use of tobacco by women. It did not suit all women as it did not agree with all men, but if it gave pleasure to the one sex, why sheuld it not do the same for the other. Dr Carpenter will no doubt sympathise with the Bishop ' of Peterborough, who has been rebuked by the Vicar of Brigstock, for seeking solace from the weed, his remarks certainly seem calculated to encourage feminioe indulgence in the habit. It is not a matter of con- science, but whether or not it is a desirable babit,every woman must decide for herself. Concerning ileadgear.—A fashionable woman was , recently heard to remark how glad she was hats are going to be smaller, as she would have more to spend on other things ; It is quite true hats are smaller, in fact the small hat reigns supreme, but it is doubtful if this variation on the part of Dame Fashion, is making the smallest difference to the modish woman's purse. Nothing is too rich, too costly, for the small bat, and it would seem as though milliners, having little space at their command, were determined that not an inch should go unimbellishtd. The peculiar positions of feathers, broom and brush-like ornaments, and spiky arrangements, become even more peculiar as time goes on, and a fashionable assembly when bats are worn would suggest to the uninitiated that the militant suffragists had been turning their attention to women's headgear. - - _

A Suggestion for Old Fur.—The women of to-day differ from those of yesterday in nothing so much as in matters sartorial. Twentieth century dresses are bought to be worn, not to be wrapped away in lavender and to see the light perhaps twice a year at some important function. These latter have multiplied at least threefold during the last fifty years and more dresses are c'niscepontly needed. The woman with a small allowance, who is obliged to dress smartly, if she be wise, tries advantage of the good ready-to-wear afternoon dress, which during the sales may be bought, a bargain. A little fur generally improves the appear- ance of such gown. Many women have odds and ends of peltry, for which they have no use, but if taken to a good furrier, even if moth-eaten in plaees, and shabby, seemingly, it is surprising what can now be done to restore the prestine beauty of the pelt. The compara- tively small cost of cutting and repairing, will prove a good investment and when the fnr trimming is added an inexpensive read-to-wear-dress takes on on air of distinction, and looks worth at least double, often treble, the money paid for it. Pleated and Tailored Skirts.—Pleats will make their appearance in all sorts of unexpected places in the fashions for this spring. Skirts of colours that contrast with the long tunic worn over them are pleated, the tunic being cut away in front, and showing the under- skirt. Afternoon frocks of the latest designs are plkated from throat to hem. The fulness is confined at the waist by a belt which is often of draped silk closed limier a braid motif. Evening dresses are also pleated, and frequently a tunic of some filmy fabric is worn over the dress, which shows through. Ne.turally only the softest, most supple materials are thus employed. The tailored skirt is still narrow on the hips, but wider at the hem, where a flat pleat is intro- duced, or a slight but attractive drapery extends the width. This is caught up by a large braided button or a motif.

he Russian Coat.—The popularity of the Russian tailored coat shows no sign of waning. Indeed there is every prospect of a still greater vogue, for this becoming garment as the year grows older. For the coming season tailors are making the coat rather long, and using braid in the place of fur al a trimming. Usually the collar is round, but on the newer models, the Medici has been successfully introduced. Hopsack and other open weave of cloths will be employed largely for suits with Russian coats. These materials are made up just now in Paris, in mustard yellow, and similar shades, and with black trimming are very strik- ng and becoming. Black, White and Red.—Two now shades of red deserve mention. The one may be compared to a dying flame ; it is snbdued, yet deep and rich. The ether is the exact shade of the red gladiolus, and quite as vivid. Black and white alone, or in combination, have lost none of their charm, either in the realm of dressmaking or in millinery. The black evening frock mounted on white crepe de chine, and veiled in delicate lace, is very elegant, and therefore very much worn. The black bat is as firmly fixed in the affections of the fashionable woman as the black dress. Both are becoming to most complexions and always, if well made, distinctive and smart.

WAGES.

Mr A Browne he railwayapp appeared company. it plaintiff ape tTwaiatsrsetaprteesd thenteadt it was a custom of the company tO, pay each week's wages on Friday, but wages so pal° had been made up to midnight the previous Wednesday, or, in the event of the man being on a trip, to 9 on Thursday. The plaintiff had almost completed • trip from Camden at 7.30 on Wednesday, October 21 when be was arrested on a charge of stealing an, receiving bottles. of whisky. He was convicted-, aria the company maintained that because he had failed te complete a week's work the plaintiff had forfeited tb. whole of his week's wages.

His Honour said the -company relied upon the prim ciple, well-known in law, that, where there was 0 weekly hiring, if a person, through no fault of hill employer, failed to complete his week, he was Il4° entitled to any wages. In this case, however, th/ contract, differed from the ordinary contract of service in which each side was bound together, the one t°,, work and the other to employ for at least a week. Tb,v company were not bound for any particular period, because the widest possible power was given to the superintendent to dismiss the man for any cause , thought sufficient. The workman, however, cool not leave without giving a month's notice. In his agreement 'with the company, tb plaintiff acknowledged: "I shall not be entitle! to any wages from the date of my dismissal or for 07 time during %%bleb I shall be suspended or absent fro!! duty, for any cause." The company, however, did OP; claim any right in that document to prevent the plaina!! receiving any money during a broken week. Moreovori the wages sheet, made out after the company knew,o' the man's arrest, showed that £2 5s 6d was due to hilPi It was, however, contended that the sheet NM It properly made out. When the whole of the cirour9:, stances were known to the company, one official wror, to another stating : " You must summarily disrall" tlø, fireman Thompson. He must not be paid for any be may have been suspended." There was not Pi faintest suggestion that he was not entitled to paytne° for any portion of the week. His Honour decided that the contract was for succeirn sive trips, and inasmuch as the last trip bad not bepe/ completed, he gave a verdict for the plaintiff for P costs on the highest scale. Leave to appeal was giveti.

LIVERPOOL AND THE SLUM QUESTION. Alluding to the recent poll on the Lymm Parlis- raentary Bill for the purchase of the waterworks, lake, la" adjoining lands, the Rev G B Thurston (rector of rjlno3), writes as follows in the Lymm Parish :magazine for February :—" I feel I can hardly refrain, In this parish record, from touching shortly on the Ruitter that stirred the whole population of Lymm and "Ightrington to the depths last week—the Parlia- raentary Bill, in which the purchase of the lake and 'waterworks was involved. PADGATE SEWERAGE SCHEME.

Matter Temporarily Deferred.

For the present, the Padgate Sewerage Scheme, which has caused no small amount of sensation in the district, has been deferred. This decision was made known at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council held in Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday afternoon, Mr G Mather presiding. There were also present :—Rev A M Mitchell, Messrs A Brooks, W Furness, P Longton, J Backhouse, J Sutton (clerk), B S Newcombe (surveyor), H H Burnage (assistant surveyor), T Kerfoot (inspector), and Dr Sephton (medical officer). The discussion commenced on the Clerk reading the following resolution from the Padgate Parish Council which had been read at a meeting of the General Pur- poses Committee on January 29th :—" That this meeting protests most strongly against the proposed sewerage scheme, sets forth that the health returns for the township for many years past have been most satisfactory and that there have been no diseases of an lepidemic nature beyond the usual children's diseases which occur in the most up-to-date sanitary districts; that an increase in the rates of anything like the sums mentioned, would mean the depopula- tion of a very. large portion of the township and there- fore we consider it would be a burden on those left to pay the rates ; that any accumulation of sewage matter in the brooks in the township could be removed by a systematic cleaning of such brooks at a small expense to the township. In view of consideration by a parish meeting and on consultation with the medical officer upon the matters set forth in the protest it was recommended by the Committee that the subject be deferred for the present. Dr Sephton said they hadhad that matter before them for many years and it had always been passed over and nothing had been doue. He had reported to the Local Government Board year after year that a better sewerage system was required for Poulton-withFearnhead. Cleansing the ditches was a nasty Way of dealing with the difficulty and he did not think it would be satisfactory. He thought they had gone so far in this matter that the Local Government Board would not allow them to escape from it. It would be injurious to the parish and detrimental to the health of the place if they did not proceed with some sewerage scheme. The health returns, be must admit, had been very fair. If they allowed all these filthy ditches to be mere open sewers, in the hot summer mouths, when they were giving out pestilential gases and poisoning the air, the result might be that an epidemic would spring out. They would bare the people affected all through- out the district, and.what would be the few thousand pounds compared with the health of the district and the deaths which would accrue? Whilst he very much objected to putting the ratepayers to any expense he thought that they could get a scheme which would be less costly than the one before them. He thought ' they could not do better than go on with some scheme, The Rev A M Mitchell; • You don't contradict the statement they make in regard to the health of the parish? Dr Sephton : The health is good. Rev Mitchell : And has been for some time ? , Dr Sephton: Yes. They make a statement on the I general state of health of the district. Mr Furness said the sewerage system aas very unsatis- factory at Padgate. He suggested that they reconsider the advisability of minimising the scheme which they bad had before them for so long.So far as some provision was being made for Padgate, they ought to go on, and not halt by the way. The doctor had said that the matter rested on the shoulders of the Council and he did not think it was fair that they should talk of solving the question by means of the ditches. The large scheme was abnormally high and very unreason- able and such an enormous scheme should not be put on the shoulders of Paegate. He suggested that they reconsider the scheme which had been formulated and which they had adopted. The Clerk : Not so far as that. Mr Furness: To retract and make some improve- ments in the scheme.

Dr Sepliton : We are not allowed by the Local Government Board to retract entirely. Mr Furness : I say the scheme is absolutely out of all reason and I should say so if it came in my district. I say in face of your (the medical officers') report that we cannot back out of it, but that we must have some scheme. I suggest that this matter be deferred and that we get a more minimised scheme. Rev Mitchell : Is there not a difference of £B,OOO between the two schemes? Mr Brooks : Between the whole scheme and the part scheme. Mr Furness: For the scheme we are getting I don't think the cost is excessive.

The Chairman : If you take a portion of the scheme the pumping has to be done just the same. The plant n ill have to be laid down just the same. The small scheme will run us in for from 4s to Gs in the S.:. People who were not tifd would clear out and that would mean empty preperty and the rateable value would be less and we should have the County rate to pay.—No man in his common senses would attempt to build in a township where the rates were lOs in the R.

Dr Sephton : And no-one would attempt to put up houses where there is all sewage surrounding his building. The Chairman : They are putting them up. Dr Sephton referred to a house so surrounued and the Rev A M Mitchell asked what had the inspector to say to that.

The Inspector: Ic is an open sewer and we have to clean it out.

Mr Furness suggested that a modified scheme should be put before the parishioners and let them consider it at a parish meeting.

WE PUT NO DROPS OR DRUGS IN THE EYES.

The Examiner, Saturday, February

Mr Brooks: Do I understand that Mr Furness is quite clear that we have a small scheme and a large one? Is it the small scheme Mr Furness wants putting before the Parish Council. _

Mr Furness : That is what I mean, the small scheme The more modifleri scheme. _ _

The Rev A M Mitchell : The small scheme is a very big one. The Chairman: We thought you meant anothe scheme. __ _ _

Mr Furness : Submit a small scheme to them.

The Chairman : I don't think the Local Goverment Board will force us to spend£ls,ooo on a rateable value of .£B,OOO. Mr Furness : it is out of all reason. After referring to a similar country district having to adopt a scheme which increased the rates and then havidg to try another because the first %Nag unsatis- factory, the Chairman said they could get two men to clean the ditches at a wage of about £5O each per year. The Rev A M Mitchell : Yes.

Mr Brooks: £5.0 -each, and for a cart as well. The Chairman: I never mentioned cart. The farmers would cart It away willingly. If that sewerage scheme was put on Padgate then God help Pad gate. A parish meeting could be called and the decision would be stronger than that at the Parish Council. Rev A M Mitchell : Sometimes we have little surprises. The Chairman : We have congregated in Padgata from 150 to 20 children, which no other parish has. They are at an epidemic age, but there has never been an epidemic yet. Mr Furness : The conditions existing there are getting more acute year after year. The Chairman : Where are they getting acute ? Mr Furness: We have evidence of that from the medical officer's report and in the letter from the Local Government Board some time ago. As time goes on, unless you make some provision, you are going to have a condition of things which will not reflect what you have had in the past. The Chairman : That is 'meeting trouble half way and putting a cloud across. Mr Brooks submitted that the minutes be adopted, and this course was taken; the matter being deferred until a parish meeting had been held and after recon- sideration of the matter by the Council.

ORFORD.

CHOIR SOCIAL—A very successful social and dance was held in Orford School on Friday evening, the 31st ult. Thera was a good attendancs and evory thing went off without a hitch. The time passed pleasantly, and rapidly, the various games and competitions being much enjoyed. Mr R Barlow kept the company in a good humour with his humerous songs, including the " Ragtime Sea." Mr J Swinton was M.C. for the dancing which was kept up until midnight. Mr R Wilson officiated at the piano. The proceeds are to go to the choir fund for providing music, &c.

STOCKTON HEATH.

PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.—The Parochial Committee held its monthly meeting on Tuescay evening at the Hall Cafe. Mr G Pilling presided and there were also present:—J H Clarke, F Pearson, W Mclntyre, J Hallows, J Whitley, le Rose, J McDermid, H J West- brook, J R Wood, C Hallows, G 3 Brown. G F Ashton (clerk), Stanley Wright (surveyor), J Farrington (inspect or).—The County Surveyor wrote with reference to bad flooding of the road at Twenty Steps Bridge, that he was still in communication with the engineer to the Warrington Corporaticn and was going to take the matter up with the Ship Canal Company's engineer. As the committee were aware. the Ship Canal Company were responsible for the portion of the road between the gates and the abutments of the bridge, and the Warrington Corporation for the light railway track. 'Mr Pearson raised the question of the condition of Alexandra-road, which he described as a scandal and a disgrace to any community. It seemed to him that there was a permanent lake in the road and it called for strong action on their part. He proposed that a committee inspect the village with a view to making application to the Local Government Board for this and other streets requiring it to be scheduled under the Private Street Works Act.—This was carried unanimously.

RIS LEY.

INDEPENDENT METHODIST CHURCH, FEARNHEAD.— A grand concert and tea was held in the above place on Saturday last. Mr E Barton occupied the chair, when a very entertaining programme was gone through. Master P Bate opened by a pianoforte solo, followed by a quartette given by Messrs Ratcliffe, Bridge, Morgan and Bate entitled "A peaceful night." The pleasant harmony and agreeable blend of voices was enjoyed. The recitation, by E Barton entitled "The women of mumbles head" was given with taste. Miss Ratcliffe very nicely rendered the song Idle words.' Mr E Bate gave a clever rendering of 6` My old Shako" for which he received an encore. The dialogue April fools" by J Houghton and others caused much merriment and showed some very good amateur acting. Miss Bridge gave an excellent rendering of "My am n I olk " for which she received an enthusiastic encore. The quartette " Allan Waters" was well sung. Al r E Barton caused much laughter by his recitation "Joe and Alice." Miss Ratcliffe and E Bate sang " Excelsior " with much gusto, and Miss Bridge gave a very sweet rendering of the song "Farewell." Mr E Bate received an encore for his song" Young Tom of Devon." Miss Ratcliffe gave tbe song Tit for Tat " in capital style. The dialogue " Barber's courtship" by A Jackson and others was well given. The tea was well attended and a very saccessful day was brought to a close by the usual vote of thanks. The proceeds amounted to £7 10s, which goes towards the purchase of a piano.—Open school was observed on the 2nd Febrnary, when various solos and recitations were given. Mr C H Harrison occupied the chair. Mr E Wood gave the address. The school- room was crowded and a very enjoyable afternoon was spent.

LANCASHIRE CONGREGATIONAL CHAIRMANSHIP.

The Rev F A Russell, of Chapel-street Congrega- tional Church, Southport, has been nominated to the chair of the Lancashire Congregational Union for next ye4r (1914). During his ministry at Crescent, Liverpool (1887-93), and since his settlement at Uhapel-street, Southport, scarcely a week has passed in which hl r Russell has not helped some church or united gatherirg in the extended area.

COSTLY lIOME RULE BONFIRE.

To celebrate the passage of the Home Rule Bill through the House of Commons, bonfires were made in many parts of Ireland. In Belfast two ardent Home Rulers, Hugh McGrogan and William Knox, entered an unoccupied public-house and ripped up the woodwork and fittings, doing damage amounting to £6B. At Belfast Recorder's Court on Thursday they were sentenced to four months and three months respectively

SHOULD THE COUNCIL BUY A NEW ONE?

Breezy Debate.

CRITICISM OF THE MUSICAL SOCIETY.

Shall the Parr Hall have a new organ? For years the music-loving public have groaned under the inflic- tion of the present apology for an organ, but hitherto all appeals for an instrument worthy of the building have fallen upon deaf ears. At last, however, the Town Council is beginning to interest itself in the question, but it is evident that many of the members will take a great deal of persuading that the provision of good music for the people should be regarded as one of the responsibilities of our legislators. At the last meeting of the General Purposes Com- mittee the following proceedings of the Parr Hall Sub-Committee were read: The instruction of the General Purposes Committee to consider and report upon the provision of a new organ was duly considered by the Sub-Committee, and alter conferring with Mr J P Reynolds, who had prepared a specification for an organ suitable for the Parr Hall, it was resolved, that in the opinion of the Sub-Committee it is desirable that a new organ be provided. Note.—Mr Reynolds submitted estimates be had obtained on his specifici- tion fronrreliabie organ builders, which ranged from £1,375 to £1,565. 'lhe General Purposes Committee resolved that the resolution of the Sub-Committee with reference to a new organ at the Parr Hall, be not confirmed.

On Tuesday evening the above minutes came before the Council for confirmation, and lively scenes were witnessed, Councillor Gough being threatened with ejection. The MAYOR, who moved their adoption, said he reserved to himself the right to criticise the minute with reference to the organ. At a former meeting of the General Purposes Committee a resolution was passed asking the Parr Hall sub-committee to inquire and report as to whether a new organ was necessary, and this was the result. He did not think it was the usual thing utterly to disregard the recommendations of a sub-committee in that manner. It did not seem courteous, and he gave notice that he would tring for- ward a special motion in favour a the provision of an organ at the next Council meeting. Councillor H ROBBRTS moved an amendment that the resolution of the General Purposes Committee, reject- ing the Parr Hall sub-committee's recommendation, be not confirmed. He said he could not imagine any member of the committee or anyone outside the com- mittee who knew anything about the condition of the existing instrument, taking exception to the sub-com- mittee's expression of opinion. That a new organ was requited everybody admitted. Councillor GOUGH : Question.

Councillor ROBERTS : I meant anybody who under- stands anything at all about it. I ought to have qualified my remarks; it is a mistake to make these assumptions universal. Continuing, he said that 99 per cent, of the people of Warrington who bad any Intelligent appreciation of music and of all that music meant were of opinion that it was quite time the present organ was removed and that a new one, credit- able to the town in quslity, should take its place. The resolution of the sub-committee only carried the matter one stage further. They were authorized to consider and report upon the provision of an organ, but they had not even gone that far. They bad merely expressed the opinion that a new, organ was desirable. Mr Reynolds had, however,assisted the Committee in form- ing an idea of what the expense was likely to be. A great deal of money had been spent—and wisely so—in Warrington in providing for the more material amuse- ments of the great mass of the people. There was not a thinking man who would grudge one farthing of that expenditure which had brought so much pleasure to it large section of the population who were very much restricted in regard to the character of their onjosment. But there was another section, largely consisting of workingmen and women who were considerably interested in all kinds of music and who:', really formed the oasis of the musical alert, f this town. A suitable organ might be purchased for a reasonable sum and, owing to ,the vast iruprovem ints made in organ-building during the last few sears, the up-keep and tuning of the instrument would 134 u compara- tively small expenditure. The MAYOR: £ i to XS a year. Aa interruption from Councillor Gough drew from Councillor WILKINsON the retort : It is not a street organ we are talking about. (Laughter). Councillor ROBERTS : Of course, the kind of organ Councillor Wilkinson has referred to about exhausts the musical possibilities of certain individuals, hut I was not Oinking of that kind of organ, nor of the usual acoompaniment—a monkey which sits on the top of it. (Laughter.) Continuing, he said there was soroethieg due to those people woo formed a very large proportion of the population and to whom the posses- sion of a new instrument and its manipulation in com- petent hands would afford considerable recreation and enjoyment. It was time the Council took their interests into consideration and complied with the recommenda- tion of the Parr Hall sub-committee. Councillor JENKINS seconded the amendment and put in a strong plea on behalf of the poor people who were unable to get music of the highest character in their own homes. It was the duty of the Council to enrich their lives by affording them opportunities of cultiva- ing refined tastes. Let them divest their minds of the idea that a paltry question of X a d should be allowed to retard their doing something which was so manifestly necessary for the welfare of the common people. If the Caunoil provided a reAly good organ, musicians from Manchester Liverpool and other centres would be only too delighted to give recitals on it. Alderman BENNETT said be felt more humiliated than he could well say to find that a committee, representing a town of the importance of Warrington, could possibly come to such s decision as the General Purposes Com- mittee had done on that question. He was glad to feel that only about half the Council were present on that occasion and that that foolish resolution was only carried by a majority of one. In a flourishing town like Warrington, so many-sided in its activities, with many rich men in its midst and with its rates not specially high as compared with other places—a town able to enjoy these little luxuries without seriously feeling the cost—he could not understand the mental attitude of those who, in a niggardly and cheese-paring spirit, refused that enjoyment to a large section of the population. All who had any refined instincts knew how soothing and elevating the influence of music was, and they also realized that that taste was not, by any means, confined to the better classes. The humblest and poorest of our citizens were able to appreciate it, too. Bowling greens and children's playgrounds— admirable and necessary as they were—did not represent the summuni bonnin of life. It should not be too much to ask the Council to spend even the maximum amount mentioned in providing an organ worthy of the reputa- tion of the town. It was humiliating to every citizen of Warrington to feel that they had a hall like that and an organ in it which was utterly unworthy and inade- quate. Councillor PEACOCK said he could not see that the proposition now before the mecting would have much effect on the resolution of the General Purposes Com- mittee. He was, however, encouraged to think that the poor people were to have the chance of benefits which they were told would be deferred till they had a new organ. At the meeting of the General Purposes Committee it was mentioned that the organ would cost .EIN) a year—("No") to cover depreciation, interest, &c. The &voR : That is for repayment of loan and interest. -

Councillor PEACOCK: That is about a a week. It was also mentioned that the organ would only be used about thirtby times a year. The MAYOR: We don't know. We haven't got it to use. . - _

Councillor PEACOCK said the poor people for whom members seemed so anxious to claim advantages, bad to pay every time they went in the Parr tHall to the Mmical Society concerts and other entertainments. Therefore, under the existing conditions, they would be excluded from enjoyment of the new organ. Alderman TINNION : They can stand under the gallery for sixpence. Councillor PEACOCK: The people whom Councillor Jenkins was talking about could not afford to pay that. Continuing, he said that if he could be assured that there would be popular organ recitals, he would support the purchase of an instrument, but if the poor

people were not going to have more advantages then they possessed to-day, he should feel strongly inclined to resist the proposal unless the £l5O a year were modified.

The MAYOR said that if a new organ were provided, they would probably get any of the local organists to give free recitals and some distinguished organists from Manchester and Liverpool would come for a nominal fee. Under those circumstances, they could afford to have free recitals or charge about one penny for admission. fhat, at any rate, was his ambition. The salient point was whether the Council admitted their responsibility of providing for those people who had musical taste, just as they had considered it their duty to provide physical recreation for the masses. Councillor PENIBERTON said Councillor Jenkins' sympathy for the musical poor was sublime and pathetic but had the Council considered the cost? The lowest estimate was £1,500, he contended it would be more fike £2,000. The repayment of principle nd interest would take from £l5O to a£l7o a year. The Musical Society, who were the chief movers in favour of a new organ, did not have very cheap prices at their concerts. The society con- sisted largely of wealthy people, and they might club together and buy an organ. (Laughter.) The Council bought "that old ramshackle thing " for them—at any rate they paid off a debt of about £2OO on it. He (the speaker) kicked against it at the time. If the Musical Society wanted an organ, they ought to buy one. They had three or four concerts a year and if two or three recitals were given, in addition, it would mean a cost of about £3O every time the organ was played. The Corporation already provided the poor people with music, free of charge, in the park. It might be news to the Council to know that the only two recreations he (Councillor Pemberton) had were music and cycling ; therefore he was talking about something he loved. The Council, however, were the custodians of the ratepayers' money and should carefully consider the cost of any proposal that came before them. There was a magnificent organ in St George's Hall, Liverp 01, yet with such a huge population to draw upon, the sttendances at the recitals were comparatively small. Alluding to the Musical Society's concerts, the speaker said

THE POOR PEOPLE STOOD OUTSIDE and watched the ladies and gentlemen In evening dress arrive. That was as much enjoyment as they were likely to derive. The Council did try popular concerts at one time, but they were a dead failure. The Cor- poration would not get one extra application for the use of the hall by providing an organ. There were plenty of fine organs in the churches and chapels and the people could hear splendid music there. As to the willingness of local organists to give free recitals, nobody would do anything for a Corporation very long without being paid for it. Councillor BRoADHURST "as one of the cbmmon people" Eupported the 'amendment. Warrington, he. said, was second to no town in the country for its instrumentalists and male voice choirs. If they followed Councillor Pemberton's lead not a single movement for the benefit of the working classes would snooped, if it cost anything. Councillor Pemberton, to his shame, opposed cheap baths for workingmea. Councillor PEMBERTON : I never objected to them. I have always been in favour of them. Councillor BRoADHuRsr : I know you did and you got your companion to vote with you. Alderman TINNION supported tne minutes of the General Purposes Committee. He said the Parr Hall was a

WHITE ELI.PHANT, but that was not because it had an inferior organ. Those who catered for the musical tastes of the people of Warrington did so in such a way that if there were two organs in Parr Hell, their efforts would be a. failure. The Musical Society would have to be put on a much more democratic basis before it was a success. At present it provided a sort of evening out for a good many people in dress clothes—that was about the sum total of the whole thing. It also gave an opportunity for others to sing in chorus who would probably never attempt to sing solo. (Laughter.) All the flowery talk they had heard was cba-ming, and enough to draw tears from a crocodile— (laughter)—but they must get down to practical common sense. The Council might putt an organ in the Parr Hall but they would not get the crowds they were expecting. Councillor BROADHURST : Make it cheap and you will.

Alderman TINNION : You may, if you do it at the popular Warrington price, "something for nothing,' but not if they have to pay. Continuing, be said he would take a great deal of convincing, before he would' vote for a new organ. Whether the wishes of the Musical Society on this matter were purely dis- interested he had not been able to decide; still, he had his suspicions. The only effect of purchasing the organ would be to support a society which unfortunately did not cater on democratic lines for the musical tastes of Warrington. They would add to the upkeep of the Parr Hall which, after all, would never pay. It bad not been proved that there was any call for an organ by the music loving people of Warrington. He assumed that if they wanted it, they would demand it, but he had not heard any such demand.

A SCENE. Councillor COUGH supported the minutes of the committee. He added that he had a suggestion for Councillor Roberts which he hoped be would take in the good spirit that he (the speaker) intended it. If Councillor Roberts had at heart the interests of the people of Warrington, and especially of the poor whom he thought the new organ would benefit.-- The MAYOR: I call you to order, I don't want you to give Councillor Roberts any suggestions except in relation to this minute, and I won't have your sugges- tions either to Councillor Roberts or anybody else. We know what your suggestions mean and I am not going to allow you to say it. Stick to the minutes. Councillor COUGH said his suggestion was that Councillor Roberts should make a gitt of an organ to the town. The MAYOR : I knew you were going to say that. Councillor GOUGH : He has made plenty of money out of the town. (Cries of" chair.") The MAYOR: Sit down. Councillor COUGH: No, no, sir. The MAYOR : If you don't obey my orders I shall have you ejected. Councillor Gouoir : Do, then. Take your own course. (Crics of "chair.") The amendment was then put to the meeting and carried by a good major:ty.

MR. G. HIGHAM.

At the anneal meeting of the Warringtrn District Past Grand George Higham of the Loyal " William Savage" Lodge was appointed Grand Master for the ensuing year. Needless to say the appointment has given great satisfaction in the District. A brief out- line of Brother Higbara's career will no doubt be of interest. Brother Higham joined the "William Savage" Lodge on the 6th. February 1892 and efficiently filled all the offices of the Lodge, receiving a Certificate of Merit for his faithful services in 1896. Brother Higher!' has also been Assistant Secretary of his Lodge for seven years and is very popular with the mem- bers. in 1904 he was President of Widows and Orphans Fund which does so much to alleviate the distressed and suffering. He also attended as a delegatQ the Annual Movable Conference held at Nottingham in 1912, also saveral Conferences in connection with the Lancashire and adjacent districts. He was also a member of the Committee appointed to carry out the arrangements with regard to the Centenary of the Order, when an excellent social function took place in the Parr Hall in commemoration thereof. Bro. Higharn has received congratulations from all sources, and the wish is that he may enjoy good health to help him to carry out his duties, which will he exceptionally heavy,as to in addition the voluntary side of Oddfellowship, the National Health Insurance will require every assistance to ensure the working of same satisfactorily.

IIR. T. P. BRADSHAW.

The newly-elected officers of the Warrington district paid their first official vi.it to the Prince of Wales Lodge on Saturday, N G %V Boardman presided and was supported by PGMG Higham, PDGMH Lawrence, IPPGM H Whitelogg, Prov CS Frank Webster, PPG MI Massey, PPG NI TP Bradshaw, PP G :11 F Freeman and PPGM Peter Lester, together with many past and present lodge officers and visiting brethren. Two interesting presentations took place, the first being that of a past officer's certificate to Bro T Newnes on the completion of his term of office. This was made by Prov G M George Higham, who spoke in very complimentary terms of P G Newnes' services to his lodge, and also of the willing assistance he heti always given to any of the lodges in the district I who asked for his services in any social event. PG M IHigham said the "Prince of Wales" Lodge was always fortunate in having very capable officers, and the fact that it was the largest lodge in Warrington, now having upwards of 630 members, showed that it had loyal and enthusiastic workers. As long as they could show such a splendid balance sheet as the one presented for last year they need not fear Competition under the Insurance Act. (Applause). Deputy P U M Harry Lawrence, on behalf of the members of the lodge, next presented PPG M TP Bradshaw with a silver tea and coffee service as a nArk of their appreciation of the good work he had done for the lodge, and also in the district for a considerable number of years.—Deputy PU NI Lawrence said nothing could give him greater pleasure than the duty he had bPvn called upon to undertake that night upon this the first visit to his own lodge since he had been elected Deputy Prov. Grand Master. As the members knew, they had decided to close the social fund of the lodge, which was inaugurated some nine years ago by Bro. Bradshaw and of which he had been continuously president. The fund had been of incalculable assistance to the lodge, but owing to the additional monetary obligations placed upon the members in consequence of the Insurance Act, the Lodge Management Committee bad decided to forego the contribution to this fund in future. In P.P.G.M. Bradshaw they had had one who, from the time he was initiated an Odd fellow, had worked hard and given of his best for the Order, and he was glad to know there were ether members of the lodge who were emulating him. (Applause). lie had also, by his intimate know- ledge of the Insurance Act, been of the greatest assist- ance to his lodge, and also to other lodges in the Warrington district. (Applause). P.P.G.M. Bradshaw and P.G. Newnes suitably replied.

St. John's Presbyterian Guild.

The above Society met on Monday evening. Miss Moore presiding. The evening was devoted to "Favourite Authors," short introductions and selections were given from the works of Charles Dickens, Oliver Wendell Holmes, Michael Fairless, Ella Wheeler Wilcox and ,James Russell Lowell and were much enjoyed, the various contributions being extremely varied and interesting.

LUCKY LOSS IN WARRINGTON.

To speak of a lucky loss seems a strange anomaly. But after reading this Warrington resident's experi- ence we shall be the first to admit that the loss was p, lucky one indeed.

Mr. J. Milner of 131, Foster-street, Orford-lane,. Warrington, says : —" For about three years, off and on, I suffered from shooting pains in my back, and after I had been sitting for any length of time I found it difficult to get up out of my chair. When I stooped the pains would ,dart across my back like knife stabs. "I had nasty headaches, which made me feel miserable. There was distress in relieving the bladder ; the water was painful and full of a dark, heavy sediment. " I tried many med;cines, but never seemed to be able to get any permanent benefit till I used Doan' backache kidney pills. Three boxes of these pills drove every symptom of kidney complaint from my system, and also improved my general health. That is now about six months ago, and I have not felt the slightest sign of a return. Doan's pills are a splendid kidney medicine, and I have told many people about them. (Signed) J. MILNER." Price 2s. 9d. a box, 6 boxes, 13s. 9d.; of all dealers, or from Foster-McClellan Co., 8, Wells-street, Oxford- street, London, W. Don't ask for backache and kidney pills—ask distinctly for Doan's backache kidney pills, the same as Mr. Milner had.

THE BLACK WAT,H FOR SHOEBURYNESS.

After being stationed for 16 months at Edinburgh Castle the 42nd Highlanders (the Black Watch) left Edinburgh on Thursday night for Aldershot, and will later be sent to Shoeburyness.

PAPER FROM NILE WEED. Successful experiments have been made, rays the "Near East," in producing from the " sudd," the river growth of the!'upper reaches of the Nile, a pulp that is suitable for making a high-class paper. The discovery is expected to have an important effect upon the paper Musty and the future of the Sudan.

WHERE THERE'S SMOKE THERE'S WASTE. Sir,—ln the Jottings of an Old Journalist last week reference is made to the proverb "Where there's muck there's money," and an alternative is suggested "Where tht-re's smoke there's money." The saying is literally true, but not in the sense used. Smoke represents value or money it it true, but it is evidence oi money being wasted not made. In the light of what is now known about smoke it would be more to the point to say" Where there's Smoke there's Waste." It is very difficult to get this fact into the minds of many manufacturers, and town Councillors, and even Mayors. The true inference which ought to be drawn from the appearance of bla:k smoke is either that the firm is doing so well generally that they can aff rd to he wasteful in this, or it is doing so badly that it cannot afford to go to the expense of adopting the necessary means to check the waste. In view of the known facts in regard to power production the attitude of mind of all who observe the production of black smoke ought not to be that of congratulation that money is being made, but regret that money is being wasted. In the works with which lam connected we have been able to cut down the fuel cost by at least one-third, and this simply by adopting means which have enabled us to convert into power by better com- bustion smoke which formerly went to pollute the atmosphere. After having swept my own doorstep clean I feel very righteous and disposed to join in the crusade to induce others to clean their steps or rather to clean the air.

THE RECTOR AND NONCONFORMITY.

- - - Sir,—The Rector's friends must feel some uneasi- ness at his ventures into the realm of Listory,especially when his words gain the publicity accorded to them in your last issue. One may be permitted without bitter- ness to say a plain word on the subject of the Rectox's comments upon the first Nonconformists. Canon Willis tells us that his predecessor Robert Yates " resigned " his Rectorship. Mr A Mounfield, in his History of Nonconformity, says that he was ejected by methods of studied cruelty, and that the Bishop's document which formally ejected him instructed the churchwardens to forcibly prevent his entering the church and to treat him `• es though naturally dead." If Canon IN illis reads this as a resignation" it is a curious reading of history. The Rector has been at much pains to tell us that Robert Yates bad no right whatever to the living. One feels obliged to ask what constitutes the right. Was not the living presented to Robert Yates by Mr Ireland in exactly the same way that it was presented to Frederick W Willis ? Presumably Canon Willis means that he had no right there because he was a Presbyterian. But the Church of England was so framed under Queen Elizabeth's guidieg hand as ts allow ministers of either Puritan, Presbyterian or Catholic opinions. Allowance was made for their varied views, as any student of the Prayer Book can easily discover. It is too late in the day for Canon Willis to assume that because all or almost all save High Churchmen have been driven out that no one else save he and hfs fellow " Catholics " had ever any right to a place in the Church or to enjoy its endowments. I have reason to know that Mr. Mounfield's " History " has been most carefully examined by High Church historical experts. But up to the present no challenge has been given to any statement therein made. If the narrative is incorrect in any detail why does not the Rector cfler a contradiction.? That would be straightferward al.d the result might be interesting.— Yours, VEVIAS.

THE PARR HALL ORGAN. Sir,—l regret to see in your issue of Saturday, that the General Purposes Committee of the Warrington Corporation, decline to adopt the recommendation of the Parr Hall Committee, that it is desirable that a new and efficient organ should be erected in the Parr Hall. There is no doubt at all that such an organ is badly needed and it is not creilitable that such a town as Warrington should be without one. The present organ is practically useless. It is an old instrument, first constructed in 1810 ; it has been rebuilt twice at least, with additions, and has been patched up till nothing more can be done with it. Its principal wind supply has collapsed, its Great Organ soundboard is falling to pieces, its pipes are falling down from their old age and weak construction and it was only by scheming and patch work that I was able to get Messrs Gray and Davison to render a small portion available for the Musical Society concert two years ago. A good modern organ is of greatest importance to the Musical Society, for as things are, it is impossible to give the rublic many important works. I do not for one moment contend that this fact in itself, constitutes a sufficient reason for providing a new organ, but it is a powerful reason, for no-one can deny that the town has largely benefited and still benefits by the society's work past and present. To say nothing of the professional talent which the society has been the means of bringing forward, I maintain that there is hardly a Church or Chapel choir in the town which is not the better for the Society's existence and work. Apart however from the Musical Society,an organ is very badly needed. I am informed that the Parr Hall does not pay and that this is held to be reason against putting in a good organ, but I contend that such an installation would do much to help matters, for it would certainly bring in additional income. Then why should the \Carrington public, (the poorer section in particular) be deprived of the opportunity of hearing what I maintain to be almost, if not quite the finest, grandest, and most elevating of music-viz : a large fine toned organ well played? in many towns weekly or periodic ecitals are given. Manchester and Liverpool pay large salaries to 'Permanent organists and I am informed that West Bromwich a town with the same population as Warrington or thereabouts has recently appointed one at a salary of £lOO per annum. Now it is hardly to be supposed that recitals entailing such expense can pay; therefore they can only be given for the benefit of the public. Serely. such considerations apply %Nit') equal force in Warringten. There is however now no question of a permanent organist for Warrington, as I was in a position to give a definite undertaking to the Parr Hall Com- mittee that given a good organ, the services of good organists could be obtained at little or no cost and recitals given at very small expense which I believe would prove a source of income. Every year money is spent and charged on the rates to provide music in the Park and in respect of other institutions which are not paying; why then refuse to provide music in the winter when outdoor music is impossible? The fact that such a large number go to hear the music in the park (and the better the music the larger the number) is in itself sufficient proof that such music as could be given at much lep expense in the Parr Hall, with admission fees of say id to Gd were a really good organ prcvided, would he highly appreciated and draw considerable numbers. The Corporation Act 1899 "gives power to con- tribute out of the rates towards payment of hands or hands of music in the park or other public place belonging to or held by the Corporation a sum of not exceeding Id rate on the Improvement Rate in any one year". This I believe provides a sum of up to £5OO per annum out of which £7O was paid to the Bands Association according to the last annual state- ment of accounts. There is therefore ample margin for spending such a small sum as would be necessary to provide winter music in the Parr Hall. I have had several interviews with the Mayor on this question and have found him very sympathetic as also a number of the Councillors. It was at his, the Mayor's, special request that I drew up a specification and obtained estimates in order that he might have I definite facts arid figures to lay before the Committee when he brought the matter forward for consideration. I believe the impression exists in the minds of some of the councillors that the cost of the upkeep of an organ would be heavy, but this is altogether erroneous for such cost would not be more than £5 to £8 per annum,including tuniug,and any firm obtaining the Contract for the organ and the tuning would give a guarantee to this effect, damage by fire, water or misuse excepted, for a period of ten years, while if adequately protected from dust not one penny need be spent on it for cleaning for at least 15 or 20 years. It is also oon-sidered, I am told that,the Parr Hall is a very unsuitable place for an organ on account of the varying temperature and damp, but this again is

quite an erroneous impression. The Parr Hall is certainly not a damp building and the organ chamber being over the kitchen and professional rooms,can not have an atmosphere containing more moisture to any material extent than the Hall itself. What Hall, Church, or other large building has not a varying temperature and consequently varying conditions of moisture in the atmosphere? Yet one never hears of organs in such buildings suffering. I have myself erected an organ in a newly built chancel of a church near Lancaster on a tiled floor immediately after being laid and yet the organ did not suffer in the lease degree. Further than this 1 bold that modern organs built on th:.• tubular pneumatic system are less liable to be effected by damp and varying conditions of atmosphere. Warrington possesses an excellent Hall, with fair accoustic properties, thanks to Mr Parr's generosity, and such an organ as I suggest would render it much more useful and be a boon to the town. I therefore plead strongly that one be provided and if you could secure support for this plea through your columns you would be doing an excellent wotk. Personally I am prepared to do anything in my power. I have had a long and. considerable practical experience of organs and organ building and I am only sorry that my means do not allow of my utilizing this experience by having an organ built under my persottal supervision and presenting it to the town. —I remain, JOHN W. REYNOLDS, Hon. Treasurer of tbe Warrington Xlusical Society. February sth, 1913.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 8, 1913.

THE COUNCIL AND PROPERTY PURCHASES.

Sir,—Things are again coming to a pretty pass in our Borough Council. Since the last -November Electi.las, which were foolishly fought on strictly party lines, we seem to have gone back to the bad times which prevailed before 1901. The mismanagement of the debates in the Council is simply appalling. Even the Mayor himself has become possessed of the idea that he can decide what shall be debated and what shall not. This is preposterous. The law says that a Council may, if it chooses, appoint Committees, but there is not a single act of any Committee which the Council may not debate if it chooses, and no Mayor has the power to prevent it. In Warrington we make our own laws as we go on ! And the public, quite naturally, laugh at us, and understand that from the chair downw ords, we are pitiably ignorant of the laws regulating these matters. To talk of sending a matter back to a commitee, and to expect that when the matter again comes b:?fore the Corn oil it will not again be debated there, is worse than childish. A committee consists of only a small section of the Council. But every Council member has his right, by law, to criticise what this smaller section has decided to recommend. It is the ignorances of this crucial fact, on the part of our Mayor, that leads him into such ludicrous decisions, and make our Council meetings into a laughing stock. Take the recent instance of a purchase of property for which they wish us to pay the iidiculous price of years purchase. Yet the Mayor rules that the Council as a whole has no right to go into details, and that any member who chooses to do so may not gl into every figure of this purchase I But this is the only E.afeguard the burgesses have against waste of their money in the shape of bad bargains, and it is a right which, after the woeful expeziences of past years, we must resolutely maintain. We have expected much of the men who have recently been sent into the Council. They have talked at their election meetings about perfect publicity in all our proceedings, and so on. But, now that they are elected, some are turning out were dumb dcgs, who are ready to bow to whatever a handful of men called a committee may decide, and who back no the Mayor in his decisions, which have the (fleet of burking legitimate discussion. If Warrington wants to avciri the repetition of its &A scandals; if it wishes to prevent the entering into contracts in which the relatives and friends of members of the Council are vitally interested ; if it desires not again to become tl:e scorn of all who desire pure local government, it must put its foot down and stop the fooling which has of late been exhibited. I hope the burgesses will closely watch the votes and speeches of the membeis, particularly of thoee who retire next November.—Yours truly, FREDERICK T. f.A.sriFona

Battersby Lane, Warrington, February 7th, 1913.

Naylor —Ainsworth.

The wedding took place on Tuesday, at Holy Trinity Church, Much Wenlock, Shropshire, of Mr. James Andertcn Naylor, son of Mr John Naylor, of Beeston Towers, near Tarporley, Cheshire, and Howley Warrington, and Miss Frances Muriel Ainsworth, daughter of Mr C E Ainsworth, The Lyuces, Much Wenlock, Shropshire. The bride, who was given away by her father, was beauti- fully gowned in duchess° satin and carried a bouquet of lilies of the valley, the gift of the bridegroom. She was attended by two bridesmaids Miss Doris Ains- worth (sister) and Miss Irene Na3lor (sister of the bridegroom), who were charmingly dressed in white satin and wore gold bangles, the gift of the bride- groom. Mr John Naylor, jun. was at the organ and played a number of pieces before the ceremony, including Lefaure , Wely's " Offertoire in D " Salut D' Amour" (Elgar) 'and the Bridal Chorus from " Lohengrin " The bride and bridegroom left the church to the strains of Mendelssohn's wedding march. A wedding breakfast was held after the ceremony at the Gaskell Arms Hotel, to which about -10 relatives and friends were invited and subsequently the happy pair left for the south coast, where the honeymoon will be spent. Mcst of the invited guests went on to the residence of the bride's parents to view the handsome presents, which numbered nearly 200 and included a solid silver centre-piece and a pair of silver flower vases from the office, staff, .and travellers of Messrs R A Naylor, Ltd. WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

THE PURCHASE OF

MESSRS. FAIRCLOUGHS PROPERTY.

IMPORTANT ARBITRATION PROCEEDINGS.

Important arbitration proceedings will take place at the Town Hall on Thursday and _Friday next. In order to carry out the Warrington Bridge works, the Corporation must acquire the property of Messrs. James Fairclough and Sons on the Latchford side of the bridge, and it is the price to be paid for the buildings which will form the subject of arbitration.

Mr. Gordon Hewart, K.C., will appear for the Corporation and Mr L La.wrenson, K.C., M.P., will represent Messrs Fairelougth and Sons.

The arbitrators will be Mr J. H Hanson, of Huddersfield (for the Corporation), and Mr Segar Owen, of Warrington (for Messrs Fairclougb). Mr J Fowler, of Sheffield, will act as umpire.

BRAVERY REWARDED.

Medal and Certificate for School

Attendance Officer.

A little ceremony, which was probably unique in the history of the Town Council, took place at the opening of the monthly meeting on Tuesday evening, when the Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph) presented Mr J H Knowles, school attendance officer, with the medal and certificate of the Liverpool Shipwreck and Humane Scciety, in recognition of his bravery in rescuing two children from a burning cottage in Thelwall-lane. on November 20. h.

Recalling the circumstances of the rescue, the Mayor pointed out that the cottagie was a one storey building comprising a kitchen and parlour. The grandmother, aged 73, was left in charge of the children, aped three and two years respectively, in the parlour. Fire broke out in the kitchen and rapidly spread. The old lady rushed through the kitchen—the only means of reach- ing the street-- calling for help end then became unconscious. Mr Knowles happened to be passihg and, by breaking a window, entered the kitchen, but

Photo Burrell MR. J. H. KNOWLES,

the flames and smoke were so overpowering that he had to retreat. He afterwards smashed the parlour wind, w, but the room was so full of smoke, that he had to cover his head with a butcher's apron before he could venture into it. After groping about for some time he found the children, banded them out of the house, and re-gaining the street, remembered nothing more until be found hims e!f in Cairo-street about a mile away. Addressing Mr Knowles, the Mayor said he dis played courage, humanity, cool judgment and tact under trying circumstances. His concluet was enhanced by his modesty, for he did not even ,report the affair at the Education Office, but went, away quietly, expecting nothing to come of it. His Worship also acknowledged the uffo, ti of Messrs Millington, Paling, Burrows and Ackroyd, who efficiently aided Mr Knowles. Mr Knowles was warmly cheered by the Council when he a2cepted the awards. He. confessed that he felt a great del more nervous that evening than he did on the cccasion of the file. (Laughter.) lie hoped the gifts would be treasured as heir-looms and that neither he nor his descendants would do any cowardly or dishonest action to tarnish the recoid He thanked the Fire Engine Committee for their kindly consideration and honorarium and Superin- tendent Turner and the Chief Constable for their thoughtfulness. Alderman Burton and Councillor Roberts (chair man of the Education Committee) warmly eulogised Mr Knowles's heroism.

ROYAL VISIT.

KING AND QUEEN COMING TO

WARRINGTON.

INSPECTION OF CROSFIELD'S WORKS.

Warringtonians have this week heard with great satisfaction that the "good old town" is to be the starting point for the tour of their Majesties the King and Queen through Lancashire in July next. It goes without saying that a loyal welcome will await their Majesties who, it is intimated, will take the opportunity of inspecting the works of Messrs Joseph Crosfield and Sons; Ltd., soap and chemical manufacturers, Bank Quay, The King and Queen, who will stay with Lord Derby at Knowsley, will arrive in Lancashire on Monday, July 7th and will return to London on Monday, July 14th. Everyday, including the first and the last on which the journeys between London and Lancashire will be made, will be crowded. On the first day their Majesties will travel from London to Warrington and, after visit- ing Messrs. Crosfield's Works, will set out on a motor-car itinerary ;on the last day they drive through the streets of Manchester and Salford and return direct to London. On the intervening days their Majesties' plan is to set out at about ten o'clock, each morning, to motor through the towns and villages on given routes, receiving Mayors and other leading men and visiting industries, and to return to Knowsley in the evening some time after six o'clock, in time for dinner. , The following provisional programme for their Majesties ' visit has been submitted by Lord Derby, and his Majesty hat been graciously pleased to approve of it : Monday, July 7.—Leave London 11 20 and proceed to Warrington. Motor from Warrington through Penketh, Hough Green, Widnes, and Huyton to Knowsley. Tuesiay, July S. - Leave Knowsley by motor at 10 o clock, and proceed by motor through Prescot, St. Helens Rainford, Ormskirk, Southport, P,eston, Kirkham, Lytham, St. Annes, and Blackpool. Return from Blackpool by train to Huy ton, arriving about 6 10. Wednesday, July 9.—Leave Knowsley at 10 o'clock, and train from Huyton to Colne. Motor from there through Nelson, Burnley, Gawthorpe,l Padiham, Accrington, Haslingden, Rawtenstall, Bacup to Rochdale. Thence by train to Huyton, arriving about 6 15. Thursday, July 10.—Leave Knowsley by motor at 10.15, pi oceed by St. Helens, Rewton-in- Makerfield, L-igh, West Leigh, Hindlev, Wigan, Chorley, Blackburn, Damen, Bolton. °Thence by train to Huy ton, arriving about 6 15. Friday, July 11.—Visit to Liverpool and Bootle. Opening (If the new dock. Saturday, July 12.—Leave Knowsley at 10 o'clock by train to Ashton-under-Lyne. Tnen by motor, passing through Oldham, Middleton, Castleton, Hey wocd, Bury, Radcliffe, Farnworth, Tyldesley, Atherton, Ashton-in-Makerfield, St. Helens, to Knowsley, arriving about 0 o'clock. Sundt.y, July 13.—Attend church parade of Liver- pool Territorials in St George's Hall, with march past of troops afterwards. Monday, July 14.—Train to Manchester. Drive through Manchester and Salford,and leave at such an hour as to arrive at Euston at 7.30. Addresses will be presented by the Corpor- ations of Liverpool and Manchester, and by the Universities of those two cities. An address will also be presented from the Royal borough of Salford. An address will be pre- sented from the Lancashire County Council on their Majesties' arrival on the first day. This address will be presented at Knowsley. We understand that their Majesties having desired to visit industries in Lancashire, and having instructed Lord Derby to make necessary arrangements, his lordship has arranged that their Majesties should visit industries at the following places, the details of which will be given later—Warrington, St Helens, Preston, Accrington, Blackburn and Oldham. The selection is an admirable one, and fairly and fully represents the great industries which absorb the energy of Lancashire.

Their Majesties will alight at all the county boroughs, and have graciously signi- fied their intention of having the Mayor and others presented to them. Halts will also be made at the other boroughs in order that the Mayor may be presented. It will be impossible for visits to be paid to any irstitutions or industries other than those which are arranged for in the towns above-mentioned.

Lord Derby proposes to pay personal visits to each of the towns so as to arrange locally all the details of their Majesties' visit.

THE LOCAL ARRANGEMENTS. THEIR MAJESTIES TO VISIT MESSRS. CROSFIELD'S WORKS.

On taking the chair at the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening, the Mayor said he had some highly gratifying information to convey to the members. He had received a communication that morning from Lord Derby relating to the visit of their Majesties to Warrington in July. The King had been pleased to signify that he would drive through the streets of Warrington in an open carriage, and would visit the works of Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., after which the Royal party would proceed by motor to Knowsley. Warrington would be the first place to be visited in the Royal tour during July. Dr. Joseph added that lie expected their Majesties would arrive at Bank Quay Station. Councillor WILKINSON : I take it that they will call at the Town Hall.

The MAYOR replied that Lord Der by had promised to see him later and arrange ab out the details of the visit. The rest of the com- munication was marked "private and con- fidental," and the announcement he had made was the only part he was authorised to make public.
