Lancashire _Catholic Players In " King

Arthur.-.

For an amateur society which has the enterprise to go back to the tragedy of the Arthurian legend written by Mr Comyns Carr for Henry Irving (says the " Manchester Guardian ") one feels a certain anionnt of respect. On Monday night, at the final performance, to judge by the size and evident enjoy- ment to the house, the ,Committee had no reason t.) regret their choice of the play. If the play would keep drawing one's Nvicked affections rather to Lancelot than to the King, that, one supposes, was a fault which could be traced even further back than to the dramatic author. The King had the best of the verse, and Mr W Kilduff played the part with a measure of dignity, spoke his lines finely, and displayed in a remarkable degree that quality of repose and economy of gesture for which one looks so far in the ordinary amateur. But for all that he never became human, except once or twice, when his proper sentiments required that he should quarrel, and, with the best intentions in the world, the play never succeeded in condemoing Guinevere. Mrs Hickey as the Queen and Mr F E Doran as Lancelot did good ,vork in their scenes titgether, and for the rest Mrs Hickey made the hest of E. monotonously plaintive part.

===== 0002601_19130208_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR GREATEST ENEMY.

Miss A. Slack and the Influence of the "Trade,"

"Take almost every Act of Parliament—the Old Age Pensions Act, the Education Act,the Insurance Act. Sm. —and the liquor traffic meets everyone and blocks the use of it." From that statement made by Miss Agnes Slack, of London at a meeting held on Wednesday evening in Cairo-street Schools under the joint auspices of the United Kingdom Temperance Alliance and the Citizens' remperance League, one can judge to a certain extent the damaging influence of the' trade." There was a fair attendance over which Mrs Dodds, in the the absence of Mr J G Withenshaw, presided. Mr Withenshaw.wrote:regretting his inability to be present He added that he hoped we should soon see the time when legislation would be introduced into Perliament and carried, giving popular control of. the " trade " both in put lie-houses and in clubs. Mrs DODDS in her opening remarks said she could not refuse to do anything she was asked, in regard to. the furtherance of the Temperance cause in Warrington It gave a big tug at her heart and she was bound to respond. They had to keep pegging at it. That was what they were doing in Warrington and she was sure they were sowing seed which would bring forth a rich harvest. (Applause). Miss SLACK proved herself to be an eloquent advo- cate of the cause and treated her subject with much skill, firing the audience with enthusiasm. At the out- set she said it was for the Christian Church to take up the. Temperance question. (Hear, hear.) There were thousands of children in Warrington who could say they had not enough food, clothes, 6:c., because of the drinking habits of their fathers and mothers. The Wesleyan Methodist Ministers were pledged by the Conference to do all they could for the furtherance of the Temperance movement. Continuing, she said that if all the licensed liquor houses in the country were put alonside each other they would make a street a thousand miles long. Ninety per cent. of the pauperism and crime in this country was due to drink and 90 per cent, of the workhouses and lunatic asylums were there as the result of the liquor traffic. Then again half of the hospitals In Great Britain were there for the same reason. Drinking destro3ed the power of the body to resist disease. If drink was done away with, half of the hospitals could be done away M' ith and halt the diseases would vanish. Then again Dr Bernardo had said that 50 per cent, of the children in his homes were there through drink. We gave .£160,000,000 in dealing with hospitals, asylums, workhouses S:c and when it was said" Deal with the liquor traffic which was cense of all those institutions " they got an answer in the negative. She could take almost every Act of Parliament—the Old Age Pensions Act, the Education Act and the Insurance Act &e—and the liquor traffic met every one, and blocked the use of them. They must work hard against the greatest enemy of the little child, the netion, Parliament, the muninipa and the enreuy of every good Act which had been passed. (Hear, hear.) Os the proposition of Mr LESLIE seconded by the Rev II Ogden the following resolution was carried unanimously. "That this meeting earnestly calls up in the Prime Minister to introduce at the earliest possible moment the long promised and greatly needed English Temperance Bill. No measure introduced by the government will so tend to remove poverty, destitution and crime, as a bill which will give the people the control over the liquor traffic." The Rev H OGDEN said they stood for local option and local veto. Their attitude was not merely local but absolution prohibition. (Hear, hear.) He himself could not understand any clergyman or minister not taking the question up, because it was a real menace to the Sunday School. Why should they whisper it in ‘Varrington that the brewery intleence was powerful ; they sheuld challenge them into the open. (Applause.) Mr LESLIE proposed a hearty vote of thanks to Miss Slack for her eloquent address and to Mrs Dodds for presiding.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Patrons of the popular Barbauld-street house are highly satisfied with the fare provided this week and needless to say the success of Fred Karno's Co. in "The Hydro" is common talk. There is an abund- ance of burlesque comedy, the major portion falling to the lot of Syd Chaplin who is a comedian with ideas as to what an audience requires to send it into hysterics, In the role of Nick Sharp, the detective, he has ample scope and makes the most of his opportuni- ties. He is well supported by a number of clever comedians and comediennes. In the bath scene all unite in one endeavour to keep the audience rolling with boisterous laughter—and they are bighlr successful. Elsie Ellis is a captivating comedienne with a breezy style, and cannot tail to please. One of the smartest shadowgraphists we have seen, is Charles, Torbay who appears in" comedy in the shade." Jack, Stocks, described as the man of mirtla,is given a hearty reception and is very popular in "The Jockey's dream." Oee praiseworthy feature is the absence of "chestnuts," all Stock's humour being up-to-date, clean and witty. Belle Davis and Cracker Jacks are fairly successful in a rag-time scoria "Southern pastimes" although to a lirge extent their appeal is made to toe popular parts of the house. John Earl, the hatedian, appears in a novel act and is well received. 'The hypnotic detective" is a clever picture shown on the bioscope.

Mr. James Kiddie, who was associated with the, successful pantomime which created such a furore in the Parr Hall a few years ego, will present the refined operatic spectacular and genuinely funny pantomime "Cinderella." .i!t. number of popular variety artistes will appear, including Miss Lily Ennis as the Prince," Will Kirk, Leo Bliss, The Lisando Brcs., The Saxon Trio, The Sylphide troupe of lady dancers and a powerful chorus and trained ballet.

Presentation to Lady Greenell. Lady Greenall yesterday received a birthday pre sent from the presidents and secretaries of the various, ward committees of the Women's Unionist and Tariff Reform League. It took the form of a gold enamelled badge of the League, jewelled with diamonds. Mrs Rylands, of Massey flail, made the presentation at the Conservative Club, in the presence of a large gathering.

Samples of goods required may be seen and speci- cation and form of tender may be obtained from the undersigned. The contractors whose tenders are accepted will be required to observe the recognised customs and con- ditioris as to the rate of wages and working hours pre- vailing within the district. Tenders, securely fastened, must be addressed "The Chairman, Sanitary Works Committee, Town Hall, Watrington," endorsed "Tender for Depot Materials' "and delivered not later than 10 a.m. cui Monday, 17th February, 1913. The lowest or any tender will not necessarily be accepted. Longford Depot, Warrington. R. WILSON. sth February, 1913. 6 Manager.

COUNTY BOROU3II OF WARRINGTON.—The SANITARY WORKS COMMITTEE of the County Borough of Warrington is prepared to receive TENDERS for the supply of the Committee's require- ments during the twelve months ending March 31st, 1914, viz.:— CLEANSING DEPARTMENT. , Bolts, Nuts, and Washers, Brushes, Disinfectants, Shovels, Wrought Iron, Wheeiwright's Timber, Rubber Rings, Workmen's Slops, Horse Hire, Steel Ashbins Steel Pails, and Provender. Parties wishing for Tender forms will please specify those materials only for which they propose to tender. Specifications and samples may be seen and form of tender and all further information obtained at the Office of the Cleansing Superintendent, Howley, on and after Monday, February 10th, 1913. Tenders to be addressed to "The Chairman, Sanitary Works Committee, Town Hall, Warrington," endorsed " Nightsoil Department—Materials," securely fastened, must be delivered not later than Monday, February 17th, 1913. The Contractor whose tender may be accepted will be required to observe the recognized customs and con- ditions as to the rate of wages and working hours pre- vailing within the district. The Committee reserve , the right to divide, the Contract in any way they may think fit, and do not bind themselves to accept the lowest or any tender. Central Sanitary Depot, JAMES J A PARFITT, Howley, Cleansing Superintendent. Warrington, Feb. 6th, 1913.

other such spices. Mustard perhaps is the only exception. Given very judiciously this condiment is good, but even then it should only be given when the weather is very severe. A like remark applies to the use of maize. This grain is a great internal fattener and should be used with precaution. Twice a week is quite often enough to use maize with breeding stock. A plentiful supply of animal food and green food are, however, essential. Lean meat boiled and chopped fine is a great egg-producer. Where green food is at a premium you may find a good substitute in mangolds and turnips. These should be cut in halves and placed in the cleanest quarter of the pen. A good supply of fresh clean water every day should not be oveilooked, and see also that the grit box is never empty, especially wilen fowls are kept in close confinement. CARE OF THE EGGS. Eggs that are being stored for the purpose of incubation should be stored in a cool room but free from keen draughts. These should not be put down in a careless manner as often enough by so doing the germs are effected. If you do not possess an egg tray, get a shallow box, fill it with bran, then place the eggs into the bran, I,trge end upwards. By this system the gerEn in the egg is kept in its proper and natural position. •

way to a kind of heaven upon earth. The struggle for success in life was not, of itself, sufficient test of fitness. There were qualities of genius, high-mindedness and imagination which often proved a drawback to people in the fight for worldly success. Of course, be was not saying that honesty, on the whole, was not the hest policy. In the notion of ideal harmony and the discovering of the broad conditions on which it depended, they learned to form a real conception of what the social good really was.

ST. HELENS. --- ST. HELENS MAN FINED FOR BETTING. John and Susan Harrison, of tB, Victoria-street, were charged at St. Helens on ;NI onday with using their house for betting.—Mr P C Kelly, who defended, pleaded guilty on behalf of the man, but not guilty for MrS Harrison. Mr Kelly, for the defence, said that Harrison was a hard-working collier, who had been trustee of the St. Helens Collieries Miners' Lodge, and for fourteen years secretary, but he was now unable to work every day owing to suffering from a miner's disease of the eye known as " mysta_lnans." He was induced at the 13... ginning of the last flat-racing scascn to take bets fa another man, hut since September he had been on his own. He, however, found the business so unprofitable that he decided some time ago to abandon it. Harrison had several times saved people from drowning in the canal, and assisted fellow-colliers in difficulties. Ile asked the police to bear out this statement that during some years Harrison had been very useful to the police, and had helped to protect constables against the violence of an infamous " black gang " which formerly infested Gerard's Bridge, where he resided.—lnspector Holding, at the request of the Bench, said that thiS statement was true, and Police Constable Smith said that in 1894 Mrs Harrison saved him and rescued 111/0 from a very dangerous position in dealing with a mob. The Bench, in view of Harrison's good character, fined him only £5 and costs. They dismissed the charge against Mrs Harrison.
